3 Network Fabrics

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why can the network connecting accelerators matter more than the accelerators themselves at scale?

A single accelerator can perform trillions of operations per second, but distributed training requires devices to exchange results before dependent work can proceed. When network capacity cannot keep pace with computation, accelerators sit idle, and adding more devices can increase the waiting instead of shortening the run. The resulting cost extends beyond the network equipment: an inexpensive connection can leave expensive compute capacity unused throughout a long experiment. Single-node intuition is insufficient because data that once traveled through a local interconnect must now cross cables, switches, and shared paths. A fast connection between two machines does not establish that the same performance will hold when the entire fleet communicates at once. The arrangement of those paths determines which groups can exchange data together and where competing transfers accumulate. Large transfers need sustained bandwidth, while frequent dependent exchanges can remain limited by the time required to start and complete each message. A delayed participant can hold up others even when most links appear healthy. Network design therefore has to follow the workload’s communication pattern, and validation must reflect the traffic that will actually run. Choices about placement, sharing, and failure recovery affect whether available bandwidth becomes useful progress or recurring delay. In C³ terms, the fabric connects compute through communication, while coordination determines when the fleet must wait for that communication to finish.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how synchronized collective traffic inverts data-center assumptions about flows, loss, and oversubscription
  • Model network communication cost using the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) framework and identify bandwidth-dominated vs. latency-dominated regimes
  • Compare high-performance transport protocols by latency, loss-recovery behavior, and operational complexity
  • Compare network topologies using \(\BisectionBandwidth\), hop count, and the workload’s collective communication pattern
  • Evaluate congestion-control mechanisms and their impact on tail latency during distributed training
  • Compare network partitioning and traffic-isolation mechanisms for concurrent training and inference workloads
  • Diagnose network performance bottlenecks using RDMA counters, link-level telemetry, and bandwidth testing tools
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

3.1 The Synchronization Backbone

One slow link stalls every peer that waits on it.

Compute Infrastructure traced the bandwidth hierarchy down to the node boundary, where each GPU’s NVLink bandwidth narrows to a single external network port. How much that narrowing costs depends on what the training job must push through the port, and how often. Consider the running instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), a 175B-parameter GPT-3-class dense transformer partitioned across the 1,024 GPUs of the reference cluster. Each training step must combine gradient contributions so that corresponding model replicas apply the same update. This operation, called AllReduce, returns the combined result to every participating worker. The model has 350 GB of gradient data at two bytes per parameter, the 16-bit gradients of mixed-precision training that this chapter assumes throughout (FP32 gradients would double the figure). That model-wide figure is not what any one GPU sends. When a reduction partitions the gradient across all 1,024 ranks, each rank owns a 1/1,024 share, a few hundred megabytes, which is the gradient-shard size the transport examples below use; the job’s tensor, pipeline, and data-parallel split (Distributed Training) sets each GPU’s actual payload. Worked examples that switch to another model, precision, or payload state their parameters explicitly. A delayed transfer on the step’s critical path can leave the other 1023 GPUs waiting at a job-wide synchronization boundary. The fabric’s contribution to training time therefore depends on which transfers the next computation needs and how much of their cost remains exposed. In that role the fabric is not auxiliary infrastructure; it is the synchronization backbone that decides whether the cluster trains as one machine.

Within the multi-tier fleet hierarchy framed by The Fleet Stack, the fabric turns isolated accelerator nodes into a coherent training system. Accelerators, power delivery, cooling, racks, and pods define what each node can compute in isolation; the fabric determines whether the nodes can act together before communication cost overwhelms computation. That design space organizes into a five-level model of co-dependent tiers, developed in section 3.2.1.

At scale, communication cost can dominate computation cost. The fleet-law decomposition in The Fleet Law makes this explicit: the nonoverlapped communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime\) determines how much of each training step is exposed to the network fabric. The fabric influences the layers above it by determining whether communication can run while independent computation continues, which collective algorithms are viable, and how network partitions interact with node failures. That concurrency, communication-computation overlap, is what the \(\OverlappedTime\) term credits, because transfer time that proceeds behind useful arithmetic does not lengthen the step.

↪ How layer-by-layer scheduling achieves that overlap, and how much communication it still leaves exposed, is developed in Communication-Computation Overlap.

A collective is a communication operation performed by a group of workers. Its name specifies the result each worker receives, while an algorithm determines the messages that produce that result. The physical network fabric carries three recurring collective communication patterns:

  • AllReduce: Combines corresponding values from all participants and returns the same result to each; synchronous training uses a sum, followed by normalization, to average gradient contributions across workers.
  • AllGather: Collects a distinct portion from each participant and returns the concatenated collection to every participant, as required when reconstructing a sharded parameter tensor.
  • AllToAll: Lets each participant send a distinct portion to every other participant, an exchange central to expert parallelism when workers host different expert subnetworks and must route tokens to their assigned destinations.

Collective communication algorithms orchestrate these patterns, while the fabric layer supplies the physical wires and switches that carry them. The distinction matters because the fabric’s physical properties (bandwidth, latency, and topology) determine which patterns are efficient and which become bottlenecks.

↪ The full set of collective primitives, with the output contract each one guarantees, is developed in The six core primitives.

Systems Perspective 3.1: The network as a gradient bus
In a single machine, the memory bus moves data between the processor and memory. In a distributed training cluster, the network fabric serves an analogous role as the gradient bus that carries contributions between workers. When those exchanges limit progress, the job encounters the network wall mapped across the pod tier in The Four Infrastructure Walls. Protocols, topologies, congestion control, and collective schedules determine how effectively the job uses the available paths. This bus has a narrow segment. Since NVLink appeared, the external ports that join machines have offered less bandwidth than the interconnect inside each machine in every generation (figure 1). How much of that gap a job actually feels depends on its message sizes, port provisioning, routing, and contention, so the link ratio bounds the problem rather than predicting a transfer time.

↩︎ Communication dominance at scale, the network wall, is established in Communication dominance and the network wall.

The reference hardware gives the gradient bus analogy a concrete scale. A single H100 has a precision-specific FP16 peak of 989 TFLOP/s and memory bandwidth of 3.35 TB/s. Each GPU’s NVLink ports together provide 900 GB/s of bidirectional bandwidth to its peers within the node. The comparison that matters is per GPU and per direction. In the reference node each GPU has its own network interface card (NIC) with one NDR InfiniBand port, so a tensor leaving the node through that GPU drops from 450 GB/s of NVLink bandwidth to 50 GB/s at the port. This 9× gap is the node boundary’s cliff that the bandwidth hierarchy of Bandwidth hierarchy already identified. The model-wide 350 GB gradient state does not all pass through every 50 GB/s port; partitioning and the collective schedule determine each port’s traffic. Converting that gap into a step-time estimate requires the workload’s local payloads and communication dependencies.

↪ The network hierarchy ratio between intra-node and inter-node bandwidth, with the reference NVLink and InfiniBand values behind it, is developed in Numbers Every Fleet Engineer Should Know.

Which traffic must cross that gap depends on how the job is partitioned. The same hierarchy keeps tensor parallelism, with its per-layer exchanges, inside the NVLink domain and lets pipeline stages and data-parallel replicas span nodes, the combination called 3D parallelism whose schedules are developed in The 3D training loop. The fabric adds one lever to that placement. Each data-parallel replica can sum gradients over several microbatches before an exchange (gradient accumulation), reducing how often it must cross the node boundary at the cost of changing the batch and update schedule.

Exposed communication leaves gaps between dependent compute phases. Two assumed training schedules on a common 230 ms axis, each containing 100 ms forward and backward compute phases, make that cost visible (figure 2). The 8-GPU intra-node case assumes a 1 ms AllReduce and spends 99.5 percent of the step in those compute phases; the 64-GPU inter-node case assumes a 30 ms AllReduce and spends 87 percent in computation. The two AllReduce durations are illustrative inputs chosen to show the effect, not values derived from the link rates above, and the percentages are shares of the schedule rather than measured arithmetic utilization.

In Fleet Law terms, the widened red block is the part of \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) that no \(\OverlappedTime\) has hidden. In the inter-node schedule it is 30 ms of every 230 ms step, about 13 percent, and that share applies to every accelerator-hour the cluster spends for the life of the run. Network fabric design therefore belongs at the center of distributed-training design rather than at its edge, and keeping that exposed window small requires rethinking the traffic assumptions that govern conventional data-center fabrics.

Self-Check: Question

  1. On an H100 GPU server, why does moving gradient synchronization across node boundaries create a severe ‘bandwidth cliff,’ and by what factor does per-direction bandwidth drop when transitioning from local NVLink to a single NDR InfiniBand port?

    1. Bandwidth drops by roughly \(2\times\) because optical conversion adds latency while maintaining similar wire transfer rates
    2. Bandwidth drops by roughly \(50\times\) because inter-node links must encapsulate packets in kernel TCP/IP stacks
    3. Bandwidth drops by roughly \(9\times\) (from \(450\text{ GB/s}\) per direction to \(50\text{ GB/s}\) per port) due to the physical distance and switching constraints of inter-node cables
    4. Bandwidth drops by roughly \(100\times\) because inter-node communication requires staging all gradients through CPU system RAM

  2. In a 1,024-GPU synchronous training job on a 175B-parameter model, a degraded inter-node link carries a required collective transfer. Explain when its delay can leave the other participating GPUs waiting, and why the link fault alone does not establish a job-wide slowdown.

  3. True or False: AllToAll collective communication is less demanding on network fabric bisection bandwidth than AllReduce because each GPU only sends data to a single designated peer.

  4. In a distributed training cluster, the network fabric functions as the global ______, analogous to the internal memory bus in a single workstation, moving parameter updates and activation tensors between accelerators.

See Answers →

3.2 How ML Networking Inverts Data-Center Assumptions

A conventional cloud network built for millions of independent web requests breaks down when a training job releases its gradient exchanges in lockstep at the end of a backward pass. Statistical multiplexing relies on traffic peaks not all coinciding, whereas synchronized workers demand the same switch paths at the same microsecond. Web services also have dependency chains and tail-latency requirements, but a bulk-synchronous training job binds every participating worker to a single shared completion boundary.

The ML networking inversion in table 1 concerns this change in traffic dependencies. Synchronous collective phases can generate correlated bursts and long-lived flows whose completion gates the next computation. Fabric validation must therefore measure collective completion under the expected load; average per-flow throughput alone does not establish that the job can meet its step-time budget.

The bulk synchronous parallel (BSP)1 model, the lockstep at step barriers already met at the scale of one node (The Node), makes the dependency in table 1 explicit. In the synchronous training example, all 1,024 GPUs must complete the required gradient exchange before the next step can proceed. A 100 ms transfer delay adds 100 ms to that step only if it extends the critical path by the full interval; other computation or communication may hide part of it. At a barrier that waits for the last required result, tail latency, the slow end of the completion-time distribution (commonly reported as its 99th percentile, p99), decides when the step ends, so at such a barrier the tail cannot be dismissed as an outlier.

1 BSP (bulk synchronous parallel): Proposed by Valiant (1990) as a bridging model between parallel hardware and software. Synchronous data-parallel training follows the same barrier structure (Goyal et al. 2017; Li et al. 2020).

Valiant, Leslie G. 1990. “A Bridging Model for Parallel Computation.” Communications of the ACM 33 (8): 103–11. https://doi.org/10.1145/79173.79181.
Goyal, Priya, Piotr Dollár, Ross Girshick, Pieter Noordhuis, Lukasz Wesolowski, Aapo Kyrola, Andrew Tulloch, Yangqing Jia, and Kaiming He. 2017. “Accurate, Large Minibatch SGD: Training ImageNet in 1 Hour.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1706.02677 abs/1706.02677.
Li, Shen, Yanli Zhao, Rohan Varma, Omkar Salpekar, Pieter Noordhuis, Teng Li, Adam Paszke, et al. 2020. “PyTorch Distributed: Experiences on Accelerating Data Parallel Training.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 13 (12): 3005–18. https://doi.org/10.14778/3415478.3415530.
Definition 3.1: Bulk synchronous parallel (BSP)

Bulk synchronous parallel (BSP) is a parallel execution model in which every worker completes a local computation phase, exchanges the data required by the next phase, and then waits at a global barrier before any worker begins the next phase, which makes the slowest participant the pacing constraint for the entire cluster.

  1. Significance: The slowest required computation or communication determines when a superstep can complete. Faster workers wait at the barrier, so a localized delay can consume idle accelerator time across the entire participating group.
  2. Distinction: BSP organizes execution into synchronized supersteps; it does not itself prescribe a learning algorithm or a particular exchange pattern. Synchronous data-parallel updates match a single-device reference only under compatible global batches, update rules, and numerical assumptions.
  3. Common pitfall: Removing barriers does not by itself establish faster time-to-quality. Asynchronous training trades waiting against stale updates, and the convergence and wall-clock comparison depends on the workload and update schedule.

↩︎ Synchronous tight coupling, the shared step barrier that lets the slowest worker pace a training fleet, is established in Independent tasks vs. synchronous training steps.

2 Elephant and mice flows: The model-wide gradient size is not the size of one network flow. A collective can split each worker’s local gradient shard across channels and transport connections; the resulting long-lived flows can still collide under ECMP when their static hashes select the same path.

The flow inversion compounds this problem. Traditional networks are designed for fairness among millions of “mice flows,”2 yet ML training is dominated by a few “elephant flows” corresponding to the gradient AllReduce. The full 175B model has 350 GB of gradient state at the assumed precision, but a sharded job distributes that state across workers. Each collective operates on its participating group and local payload; its algorithm determines how much each worker sends. Standard flow control and routing mechanisms like equal-cost multi-path (ECMP), which hash each flow onto one of several equal-cost paths, are poorly suited to this traffic pattern. A static hash cannot subdivide one elephant flow across all available links, and it can inadvertently map multiple elephant flows to the same link, creating massive congestion while other links sit idle.

The loss inversion completes the picture. TCP was designed to recover across an unreliable network, and an occasional retransmission delays only the request it belongs to. In a synchronous collective, a lost packet delays the transfer that every waiting worker needs, and some NICs that implement remote direct memory access (RDMA), called RDMA NICs or RNICs, recover by resending everything after the missing packet rather than only the lost region (Go-Back-N-style recovery). ML fabrics therefore aim to make loss rare and recovery bounded, either by preventing buffer overflow at the link layer or by building transports that recover quickly from it. Level 2 (section 3.4.3) develops both approaches.

Correlated demand also makes oversubscription, provisioning less uplink capacity at a switch tier than the downlink capacity it serves (written as a downlink:uplink ratio such as 2:1), a workload decision. A fabric with spare capacity under independent requests can become saturated when a collective releases traffic across the same cut. Designing for that traffic requires tracing the constraint from the wire through transport, topology, and congestion control to the job’s synchronization boundary.

3.2.1 The five-level model

Provisioning fast NICs or buying a non-blocking switch tier in isolation routinely fails to speed up training: a 400 Gb/s link still stalls if host TCP copies starve the PCIe bus, an unaligned rack layout exhausts the copper link budget, or static ECMP hashing piles two elephant flows onto one spine port. Tracing why a collective slows down requires following the packet across five coupled layers, from serializer/deserializer (SerDes) signaling and copper or optical reach at Level 1 to the integration of a production cluster at Level 5 (figure 3). Each level is developed at the point where its physical or protocol limit becomes the binding constraint.

Each inversion of table 1 traces back to a specific physical or protocol constraint, and the five levels locate where each one is paid:

  • Level 1: wire and link (section 3.3). Signal integrity (PAM4, SerDes), forward error correction, and the reach of copper and optics impose hard constraints on per-hop latency and cluster geometry.
  • Level 2: transport (section 3.4). InfiniBand and RDMA over Converged Ethernet (RoCE) provide RDMA primitives; the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model quantifies the latency-vs.-bandwidth trade-off for different message sizes.
  • Level 3: switch and topology (section 3.5). Fat-trees, rail-optimized designs, and dragonflies achieve the \(\BisectionBandwidth\) needed for global collectives through different structural trade-offs.
  • Level 4: fabric behavior (section 3.6). Link-level flow control and end-to-end congestion control determine whether theoretical bandwidth translates to realized throughput; adaptive routing and incast behavior determine whether that throughput survives real collective traffic (Alizadeh et al. 2010; Zhu et al. 2015; Li et al. 2019; Gangidi et al. 2024).
  • Level 5: cluster design (section 3.7). Production supercomputers like the NVIDIA SuperPOD and Meta Grand Teton integrate these layers into a unified gradient bus (NVIDIA 2023; Meta Engineering 2024).
Alizadeh, Mohammad, Albert Greenberg, David A. Maltz, Jitendra Padhye, Parveen Patel, Balaji Prabhakar, Sudipta Sengupta, and Murari Sridharan. 2010. “Data Center TCP (DCTCP).” Proceedings of the ACM SIGCOMM 2010 Conference, 63–74. https://doi.org/10.1145/1851182.1851192.

The levels are not an inventory of networking topics; they are the causal chain that turns synchronous ML traffic into infrastructure requirements. Large gradient messages first stress the wire and transport, then force topology choices that preserve bisection bandwidth, then expose congestion behavior that ordinary enterprise networks can hide, and finally determine whether the whole cluster behaves like one training machine.

The traffic pattern determines which fabric metric matters. A global reduction stresses bisection bandwidth and the slowest path; a point-to-point pipeline transfer sits on the critical path, so its full transfer time and hop count matter; an expert exchange stresses path diversity and egress balance; and checkpoint or tenant traffic stresses isolation from the synchronization path (table 2). The remaining levels develop each of these pairings in turn.

Mapping workload semantics to physical constraints shows why ML fabric design must proceed from the physical layer up. Before higher-level topologies or routing protocols can organize traffic, the physical layer must reliably deliver bits between endpoints without excessive signal attenuation or prohibitive power consumption. Level 1 begins where all fabric architecture originates: at the physical wire.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why can standard Equal-Cost Multi-Path (ECMP) routing produce persistent hot spots when a distributed ML training job has a small number of large gradient flows?

    1. ECMP hashes each flow to one path, so several large gradient flows can collide on one link while other equal-cost paths remain underused
    2. ECMP introduces non-deterministic packet reordering that corrupts the internal state of GPU tensor cores
    3. ECMP relies on centralized SDN controllers that cannot update routing tables at microsecond timescales
    4. ECMP requires all switches in the fabric to maintain complete copies of the global model parameters

  2. Contrast how packet loss is handled in traditional web-scale TCP/IP networks versus RDMA-based ML fabrics (such as RoCEv2), and explain why packet loss in an ML cluster can cause a large performance drop.

  3. Arrange the five levels of the high-performance ML network fabric model in ascending order of abstraction, from physical signaling up to cluster orchestration:

  1. Switch and Topology
  2. Cluster Design
  3. Wire and Link
  4. Fabric Behavior
  5. Transport and Performance Model

  1. Which performance metric is the primary optimization target in an ML training network fabric, and why?

    1. Average per-flow throughput, because web data centers maximize aggregate data volume across millions of independent users
    2. Jitter buffer depth, because audio and video streaming protocols require bounded inter-packet arrival gaps
    3. Maximum hop count across the core, because reducing total cable length minimizes physical fiber deployment costs
    4. Collective completion time under load, because a Bulk Synchronous Parallel barrier waits for the last required transfer; p99 transfer latency helps characterize the tail

3.3 Level 1 Wire and Link

At 400 Gb/s and beyond, the physics of signal transmission imposes hard limits on cable length, power consumption, and error rates. Signal attenuation in copper and chromatic dispersion in fiber dictate how far an accelerator can sit from its switches before communication fails. Every higher-level protocol and topology is ultimately constrained by what the physical wire can carry; these limits shape the geometry of the entire cluster. To meet throughput demands without raising the signaling rate into worse attenuation, link designs must carry more bits in each transmitted symbol.

3.3.1 Signal integrity and PAM4

Achieving 400 Gb/s and higher line rates requires balancing signaling speed against channel loss and detection errors. The symbol rate counts transmitted symbols per second; consecutive symbols need not produce a voltage transition. Raising the symbol rate raises the frequencies the channel must carry, and copper loses more signal at higher frequencies. Carrying more bits in each symbol, by using more than two signal levels, raises the bit rate without that penalty, and PAM4 is the scheme high-speed links use to do it.

Definition 3.2: PAM4 signaling

PAM4 signaling is a modulation scheme that represents two bits per symbol with four signal levels, doubling the raw bit rate relative to binary signaling at the same symbol rate.

  1. Significance: PAM4 increases the raw \(\Bandwidth\) available at a given symbol rate. The reduced separation between levels makes error-control design part of that gain. High-speed Ethernet uses forward error correction (FEC), including Reed-Solomon RS(544,514), with processing latency that depends on the physical-layer (PHY) design and implementation (Anslow 2016; Yin et al. 2022). FEC contributes to the fixed path budget modeled by the \(\MessageStartupLatency\) term in section 3.4.4; it is one component alongside propagation, switching, and endpoint processing.
  2. Distinction: At equal symbol rate and voltage swing, NRZ (Non-Return-to-Zero) binary signaling carries one bit per symbol with greater separation between levels. PAM4 carries two bits per symbol with smaller level spacing; the link must compensate through its signal-quality and error-correction design.
  3. Common pitfall: Doubling bits per symbol does not guarantee twice the usable payload throughput or a fixed latency penalty. Coding overhead, channel quality, and the chosen PHY implementation determine delivered performance.
Anslow, Pete. 2016. RS(544,514) FEC Performance Including Precoding. IEEE P802.3cd Task Force.
Yin, Shuang, Xiang Zhou, and Cedric Lam. 2022. FEC Requirements for 800GbE/1.6TbE Optics: An Operator’s Perspective. IEEE 802.3df Task Force.

The price is noise margin. With four levels packed into the same voltage swing, the gap between adjacent levels in PAM4 shrinks to one third of the NRZ gap (figure 4), making the link highly susceptible to noise. Consequently, high-speed links operate near the physical limits of reliable detection and require forward error correction (FEC) at the physical layer, commonly using Reed-Solomon RS(544,514)-family codes in high-speed Ethernet designs (Anslow 2016; Ethernet Alliance 2025). FEC processing is an implementation-dependent latency term; IEEE 802.3df operator material treats roughly 100 ns of host-side FEC with the RS(544,514) code (called KP4 in the standard) and hundreds of nanoseconds of end-to-end module latency as meaningful at AI/HPC fabric timescales (Yin et al. 2022). Because each switch on a path decodes and re-encodes the signal, the FEC cost accumulates with the number of switches a packet traverses. A path’s length is its hop count, counted as switch traversals or as inter-switch links; where the two counts differ, each example states which one it uses. A packet that traverses three switches, the leaf-spine-leaf route of a two-tier fabric, incurs a scenario budget of 300 ns–600 ns of one-way FEC latency, or 600 ns–1200 ns for a round trip. Against the 350 GB gradient of the 175B job, a few hundred nanoseconds are negligible, because bulk transfers are governed by bandwidth. The cost lands instead on small, latency-bound messages, such as the control handoffs that coordinate pipeline stages, and on collectives whose many sequential steps each pay it. This physical-layer latency sets a floor on the fabric’s fixed startup latency, formalized in section 3.4.4 as the \(\MessageStartupLatency\) term, that no software optimization can remove.

Doubling line rate without doubling the symbol rate therefore trades signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) for bandwidth, and forward error correction is what lets the link operate that close to the channel limit. Because that tighter margin leaves less room for channel attenuation, it also shortens the distance a passive copper cable can span before the link must switch to optics.

3.3.2 Reach and medium for copper and optics

Past one to three meters, passive copper attenuates high-frequency PAM4 signals beyond what even FEC can recover, forcing any link that exits a rack onto active optical cables or pluggable optics at a steep jump in cost and power. The choice of physical medium therefore determines how far a link can reach before latency, power, and cabling budgets reshape the cluster geometry, and the recurring cable and optics options differ along exactly those attributes (table 3).

The high-speed copper and optical paths considered here both include FEC, so choosing optics does not by itself remove error-correction overhead. The ranges in table 3 describe representative placements rather than fixed boundaries between media. Optical interconnects can extend the layout beyond a passive copper channel’s budget, while pluggable optics also permit separate replacement of modules and fiber. Reach, delivered latency, power, and serviceability jointly determine the choice.

Systems Perspective 3.2: The cost of distance
In an ML fleet, distance is money. Assuming two spine-layer optical links per accelerator, a cluster of 10,000 GPUs requires ~20,000 optical links at the spine layer alone. At $500 per complete link, that represents $10 million in cabling. For power, count one selected spine-side pluggable module per link at 20 W per module, giving 400 kW for those endpoints alone. Each optical link also has a module at its opposite end; this subtotal excludes those modules, switch electronics, and cooling, so it is not the full fabric power budget. The cost dictates cluster geometry: architects pack accelerators as densely as possible (70–100 kW per rack) to maximize cheap copper and minimize expensive optics.

3.3.3 SerDes, link budget, and power

Driving thousands of high-speed links across a cluster burns tens of kilowatts just to serialize bits onto the wire, rivaling a noticeable share of the accelerators’ own thermal budget. Every port depends on a serializer/deserializer (SerDes) circuit that converts parallel data from the switch application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) into a serial stream, and its power cost accumulates across the provisioned ports. For the 1,024 GPUs reference cluster, assume a full-\(\BisectionBandwidth\) fabric with roughly 3,000 high-speed links across its host and switch tiers. Count one selected switch-side port per link in this power subtotal. Each counted 400 Gb/s port is assigned 25 W, more than the 20 W pluggable-module figure in the cost-of-distance estimate because it also counts the switch-side SerDes that drives the module. The model totals 75 kW, equivalent to 10.5 percent of the accelerators’ combined thermal design power. This comparison isolates the assumed power of the selected switch-side ports, excluding the opposite endpoints and the rest of the switch chassis; it does not establish total fabric power or the fraction of measured cluster energy consumed by networking.

The link budget allocates the signal loss a path can tolerate while meeting its error target. Losses are counted in decibels (dB), a logarithmic power ratio in which every 10 dB is a further tenfold attenuation (3 dB is roughly a halving), so the losses of cables and connectors in series add directly. In this simplified accounting, a lane has a 30 dB allowance. A 3-meter DAC cable contributing 18 dB of loss and connectors contributing 2 dB leave 10 dB of margin. Higher signaling frequencies increase copper loss, so preserving reach can require more signal conditioning, a different cable, or optics. Aggregate port speed alone does not determine cable reach; lane rate, channel construction, and active components matter. The design must meet both the signal-quality target and the power budget before the transport layer can rely on the link.

Self-Check: Question

  1. What is the primary physical trade-off introduced by adopting PAM4 (Pulse Amplitude Modulation 4-level) instead of NRZ (Non-Return-to-Zero) signaling in \(400\text{ Gb/s}\) and \(800\text{ Gb/s}\) network fabrics?

    1. PAM4 triples the baud rate on the physical wire but eliminates the need for receiver equalization
    2. PAM4 doubles the data rate per symbol by using four voltage levels, but the reduced noise margin requires Forward Error Correction (FEC) with an implementation-dependent latency cost
    3. PAM4 replaces electrical copper signaling with optical coherent modulation, restricting maximum cable length to under \(50\text{ cm}\)
    4. PAM4 eliminates link-level bit errors entirely, removing the need for physical layer error detection at the cost of doubling SerDes power consumption

  2. Why can increasing per-lane signaling rate shorten passive Direct Attach Copper reach, and how should the resulting link budget constrain AI rack design?

  3. In a modern multi-thousand GPU cluster using \(400\text{ Gb/s}\) pluggable optical transceivers, why is transceiver power consumption a critical design constraint at the facility level?

    1. Pluggable transceivers require cryogenic cooling to prevent optical fiber dispersion
    2. Optical transceivers consume no power during active data transfer but draw high surge currents during idle states
    3. Counting one selected spine-side module on each of 20,000 links at \(20\text{ W}\) per module gives a \(400\text{ kW}\) endpoint subtotal, before opposite-end modules and other fabric power
    4. Optical modules dissipate heat directly into the switch ASIC die, reducing switch processing clock frequencies by \(50\%\)

  4. True or False: Direct Attach Copper (DAC) cables introduce higher propagation and serialization latency than Active Optical Cables (AOC) over a 2-meter intra-rack link because copper has higher electrical resistance than optical fiber.

3.4 Level 2 Transport and the Performance Model

Closing the physical link budget delivers raw bits between adjacent ports, but a single AllReduce across 1,024 GPUs still stalls if host software sits in the path of every transfer or if a dropped packet triggers a multi-millisecond recovery pause. Turning raw wire rate into predictable step time requires a transport layer that offloads memory-to-memory delivery and bounds loss recovery (a contract met either through a physically lossless fabric or through loss-tolerant endpoint recovery), together with a performance model that separates fixed per-message startup latency from sustained link bandwidth.

3.4.1 RDMA and GPUDirect

A conventional kernel TCP path charges the host for every byte it moves. Interrupt processing, protocol work, and payload copies consume CPU cycles and memory bandwidth that the training process also needs for data loading and preprocessing, and the charge grows with aggregate I/O as many streams or NICs drive a host at 400 Gb/s link rates. Modern TCP stacks reduce the charge with segmentation offload, receive-side scaling, zero-copy interfaces, and careful non-uniform memory access (NUMA) placement, so a single stream need not exhaust a server’s memory bandwidth. They do not remove it, and the contention becomes harder to provision predictably as nodes add NICs.

Definition 3.3: Remote direct memory access (RDMA)

Remote direct memory access (RDMA) is a networking technology that offloads transport processing to network interface hardware, allowing access to registered remote memory without per-transfer kernel processing of the payload.

  1. Significance: RDMA can reduce end-to-end message latency from tens of microseconds for a kernel TCP path to low-single-digit microseconds on a tuned fabric, shrinking the fixed startup cost that every message adds to the Fleet Law’s communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\).
  2. Distinction: Unlike a conventional kernel TCP path, RDMA offloads transport processing to NIC hardware and permits user-space access to registered memory. The resulting CPU-core and memory-bandwidth savings depend on the host, NIC, I/O path, message size, and software configuration rather than on link rate alone.
  3. Common pitfall: RDMA is an access and offload model, not one loss-recovery algorithm. Reliable InfiniBand and RoCE transports detect loss and retransmit (InfiniBand Trade Association 2000). Some RNIC generations use the Go-Back-N-style recovery noted with the loss inversion, which makes a drop expensive (section 3.4.3 quantifies the cost), while other designs support stronger selective recovery or tolerate lossy Ethernet without the link-layer pause mechanism (priority flow control) that many RoCE deployments rely on (Mittal et al. 2018). The requirement is reliable completion with a recovery tail that the synchronized workload can tolerate, not a physically lossless Ethernet fabric in every implementation.

3 GPUDirect RDMA: Introduced by NVIDIA for Kepler-class GPUs and CUDA 5.0, GPUDirect RDMA enables a direct PCIe path between GPU memory and third-party peer devices such as network interfaces (NVIDIA 2026b). Before GPUDirect, every gradient transfer had to stage through host memory, consuming CPU memory bandwidth that competes with data loading. Eliminating this bounce path is one reason overlapping communication with backward-pass computation is feasible at scale.

NVIDIA. 2026b. GPUDirect RDMA.

RDMA bypasses the kernel data path. Offloading transport logic to the NIC allows applications to read and write registered remote memory without per-message kernel intervention. For ML, GPUDirect RDMA,3 the path that dense nodes pair with one NIC per GPU (Dense node designs), extends this principle to accelerator memory (NVIDIA 2026b). Within a sending node (figure 5), GPUDirect RDMA routes an \(\PointToPointMessageSize\)-byte payload (what one participant sends, not the model-wide gradient state) along the solid green peer-to-peer path through the PCIe switch straight to the RDMA NIC, bypassing host memory entirely. Without this direct memory access (DMA) bypass, the dashed gray staging path must first copy \(\PointToPointMessageSize\) bytes from GPU memory into a registered host-memory buffer and subsequently read \(\PointToPointMessageSize\) bytes out to the NIC, generating roughly \(2\PointToPointMessageSize\) bytes of redundant host-memory bus traffic. The network still transfers the payload, and the PCIe path may comprise multiple DMA transactions; the optimization removes staging copies rather than collapsing the transfer into one transaction.

3.4.2 InfiniBand and RoCE

GPU clusters commonly choose between two RDMA transport stacks, and the decision is a reliability-versus-operations trade-off. Both stacks must keep switch buffers from overflowing under synchronized bursts, and they do so in different places. InfiniBand uses credit-based flow control. Each receiver advertises its free buffer space to the sender at the other end of the link in units called credits, and the sender transmits only into space that has been advertised. A buffer therefore cannot overflow; congestion appears as senders waiting for credits rather than as dropped packets. Ethernet was designed to drop packets when buffers fill, so RoCE deployments that want the same behavior add two mechanisms. Priority flow control (PFC) lets a filling switch port send a PAUSE frame that stops its upstream neighbor for one traffic class, and explicit congestion notification (ECN) marks packets that pass through a filling queue so that their senders slow down before a pause is needed. InfiniBand thus builds losslessness into the link layer, while RoCE assembles it from switch configuration and end-to-end congestion control (table 4); section 3.6.1 develops PFC’s failure modes.

The InfiniBand4 row reflects a protocol heritage that favors hardware-managed losslessness over Ethernet compatibility, while both RDMA stacks share the same user-space bypass across the user-space, kernel, and hardware boundary (figure 6). The conventional copied TCP/IP path on the left uses two software copies across kernel buffer boundaries and runs the protocol in software. The RDMA stack on the right traces a single amber bypass arrow from the collective communication library (such as the NVIDIA Collective Communications Library, or NCCL) and the user-space verbs API straight to the RDMA NIC, which keeps the operating system off the active data path.

4 InfiniBand: Formed in 1999 from the merger of two competing server I/O standards (Future I/O and NGIO), InfiniBand was originally designed to replace PCI as a general-purpose system interconnect. Its pivot to HPC networking preserved the credit-based, hardware-managed flow control that server I/O demanded, and that heritage is why InfiniBand provides native losslessness without the PFC fragility of Ethernet-based RDMA fabrics.

3.4.3 Reliable delivery and loss recovery

By routing the user-space verbs API directly to hardware (figure 6), the architecture decouples collective library calls from the operating system, leaving the transport hardware and fabric to arbitrate reliable delivery and packet loss. Reliable connected transports in both InfiniBand and RoCE detect loss and retransmit (InfiniBand Trade Association 2000); credits and PFC exist to make that retransmission rare (Guo et al. 2016; Gangidi et al. 2024). Losslessness is therefore a deployment contract rather than a property of RDMA as a family. Designs that operate over lossy Ethernet without PFC shift more responsibility to endpoint congestion control, packet reordering, and selective recovery (Mittal et al. 2018).

Recovery granularity therefore matters. On an RNIC with Go-Back-N-style recovery and no out-of-order retention, a lost packet invalidates every packet that arrives after it, and the sender must retransmit from the missing sequence point. The penalty is everything the sender transmitted between the lost packet and the moment it learned of the loss, so it equals link rate times detection delay. If the receiver’s negative acknowledgment reaches the sender after one 5 μs round trip, a sender at 50 GB/s has meanwhile sent 250 KB, all of which it must send again; a drop 900 MB into a 1 GB transfer wastes that window, not the 900 MB already delivered. When detection instead waits for a retransmission timeout, the delay, and with it the resent window, grows with that timeout. An RNIC with selective recovery retransmits only the missing packets, so the same physical loss produces a shorter tail (Mittal et al. 2018). Because the window is line rate times detection delay, resending it takes about as long as detecting the loss did, so a faster link resends more bytes per drop without shortening the stall. Either way the stall spans many packet wire times, and a synchronous collective exposes the slowest recovery to every waiting worker. Fabric designers can reduce that tail by suppressing drops with PFC, by using a loss-tolerant transport with bounded endpoint recovery, or by combining congestion control and admission policy so queues rarely overflow. Each choice moves state and failure modes between switches and endpoints.

3.4.4 The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) performance model

Even on a lossless RDMA path with zero packet drops, doubling the link rate barely speeds up a few-kilobyte pipeline control handoff while cutting a multi-hundred-megabyte gradient transfer nearly in half. Separating those two behaviors requires a cost model that distinguishes fixed per-message overhead from per-byte transmission rate. The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model (principle 5) decomposes message transfer time as \(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) = \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\), where \(\PointToPointMessageSize\) is the message size in bytes, \(\MessageStartupLatency\) is the fixed startup latency, and \(\LinkBandwidth\) is the sustained bandwidth in bytes per unit time (Hockney 1994). The physical topology influences both parameters through path length and available capacity. A collective built from many sequential rounds of messages pays \(\MessageStartupLatency\) once per round, so its startup cost can grow with the number of participants even when the underlying fabric provides short physical paths.

Hockney, Roger W. 1994. “The Communication Challenge for MPP: Intel Paragon and Meiko CS-2.” Parallel Computing 20 (3): 389–98. https://doi.org/10.1016/s0167-8191(06)80021-9.

↪ The same decomposition, multiplied by each schedule’s round count and traffic factor, is developed for collective algorithms in Pricing Collectives by Rounds and Chunks.

The model reveals two regimes that call for different engineering responses. When \(\PointToPointMessageSize< \MessageStartupLatency\LinkBandwidth\), the startup cost \(\MessageStartupLatency\) exceeds the transmission cost, placing small control messages deep in the latency-bound region, whereas when \(\PointToPointMessageSize> \MessageStartupLatency\LinkBandwidth\), the \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) term dominates and large gradient transfers sit deep in the bandwidth-bound region. Near the crossover both terms matter, and where a collective lands also depends on how its algorithm divides the payload into messages and schedules them across rounds.

↪ The crossover message size that separates the latency-dominated and bandwidth-dominated regimes, with a worked InfiniBand NDR example, is developed in Latency-dominated vs. bandwidth-dominated regimes.

For the modeled NDR InfiniBand path with \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s, the crossover point \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth\approx\) 75 KB. Measured startup latency and delivered bandwidth vary with the NIC, switch path, software stack, and contention, so these parameters describe a scenario rather than a protocol constant. Far below the crossover, more bandwidth removes only a small part of transfer time; far above it, lower startup latency removes only a small part. Shorter paths can lower \(\MessageStartupLatency\), while additional delivered path bandwidth raises \(\LinkBandwidth\); their value depends on the message sizes the workload actually exchanges.

Napkin Math 3.1: The alpha-beta crossover
Problem: A fabric designer is comparing a modeled InfiniBand NDR path (1.5 μs, 50 GB/s) against a modeled RoCE path over slower Ethernet (5 μs, 12.5 GB/s). The comparison deliberately bundles a higher-latency stack with a slower link, so a third path, RoCE on a 400 Gb/s link, separates the two effects. For a 4 KB control message and a 350 MB gradient shard, which part of \(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize)=\MessageStartupLatency+\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) dominates, and why does the first path help for different reasons in each regime?

Math: Apply \(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) = \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) for a 4 KB control message and a 350 MB gradient shard.

  1. Small message (4 KB):
    • InfiniBand: \(1.5\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} + 4\,\text{KB}/50\,\text{GB/s} = 1.5 + 0.08 = \mathbf{1.58}\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)
    • RoCE (100G): \(5.0\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} + 4\,\text{KB}/12.5\,\text{GB/s} = 5.0 + 0.32 = \mathbf{5.32}\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)
    • Result: Under these assumed path parameters, the NDR path is 3.4× faster because its modeled \(\MessageStartupLatency\) is lower.
  2. Large message (350 MB):
    • InfiniBand: \(\PointToPointTransferTime= \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth=\) 7.0 ms
    • RoCE (100G): \(\PointToPointTransferTime= \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth=\) 28.01 ms
    • Result: Under these assumed path parameters, the NDR path is 4× faster because its modeled \(\LinkBandwidth\) is higher. That factor is the ratio of the two line rates, so it measures the faster link rather than the transport.
  3. Same line rate (RoCE at 400 Gb/s, RoCE \(\MessageStartupLatency\)):
    • Small message: 5.08 μs, still 3.2× the NDR time, because \(\MessageStartupLatency\) alone separates the stacks.
    • Large message: 7.0 ms, essentially the NDR time, because at equal \(\LinkBandwidth\) nothing but a few microseconds of \(\MessageStartupLatency\) separates them.

Systems insight: This scenario places the crossover \((\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\LinkBandwidth)\) near 75 KB. Because gradients are megabytes to gigabytes, their transfers are usually bandwidth dominated, whereas pipeline coordination can remain latency dominated. A higher line rate produces little benefit for a message whose startup term dominates; transport processing, topology, and hop count then govern the improvement.

The two message sizes in notebook 3.1 map directly onto the 175B training job. For the 4 KB control message that notifies a pipeline stage its next microbatch is ready (\(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) \approx\) 1.58 μs on NDR), the transmission term contributes only 5.1 percent of the total, so faster links offer little benefit. For the 350 MB shard that each of the 1,024 GPUs owns from the 350 GB gradient (\(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) \approx\) 7.0 ms), transmission dominates, and doubling bandwidth to 100 GB/s approximately halves the transfer time.

Across the full range of message sizes, each path traces a flat latency-dominated plateau that bends into a linear bandwidth-dominated regime (figure 7). On the modeled NDR path, the knee at 75 KB marks where transmission time overtakes the 1.5 μs startup latency; the 100 Gb/s RoCE path sits on a higher floor of 5 μs and bends near 62.5 KB. The exact positions reflect the assumed \(\MessageStartupLatency\) and \(\LinkBandwidth\) scenario parameters rather than invariant protocol constants. On both paths, startup latency governs sub-kilobyte control handoffs, whereas multi-megabyte gradient shards sit well inside the bandwidth-bound regime.

A measured collective shows both regimes and where the two-parameter model needs care. NVIDIA publishes the full output of the standard NCCL AllReduce benchmark on one node of eight H100 GPUs connected by NVLink, sweeping message sizes from 8 bytes to 16 GB (NVIDIA 2026a). The two-parameter model can be built from two summary values of that sweep, the median time of the small messages as \(\MessageStartupLatency\) and the delivered rate of the largest message as \(\LinkBandwidth\), and set beside the measured time (figure 8). The small messages sit on a floor of about 17 µs regardless of size, and the largest messages approach 274 GB/s of algorithm bandwidth, defined as the tensor size divided by completion time. Because an eight-GPU ring AllReduce sends \(2(8-1)/8 = 1.75\) bytes across each GPU’s links per tensor byte reduced, multiplying by that factor gives 480 GB/s of bus bandwidth, the benchmark’s hardware-normalized rate on the physical links. Between the two extremes, the model places the knee near 5 MB, but the measured time runs up to 1.7× the model’s prediction for messages of 8 to 128 MB. Across that middle range, chunks are not yet large enough to fill every parallel NVLink channel and amortize per-round protocol overhead, so delivered bandwidth is still ramping toward its peak. The model is therefore close at the two ends and optimistic in the middle, and that middle range is where the gradient buckets of data-parallel training often fall.

Checkpoint 3.1: Protocol selection

Consider the 175B job on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. Its gradient exchanges move messages of hundreds of megabytes, while its pipeline stages coordinate through frequent control messages of a few kilobytes.

Beyond classifying individual transfers, the same model identifies when communication overtakes computation. A ring AllReduce passes chunks around a logical cycle of participants, in a ReduceScatter phase and then an AllGather phase, with no requirement that the physical switches form a ring. The scaling laws that push training toward more parameters (AI Scaling Laws) enlarge, in direct proportion, the gradient that every data-parallel step must reduce. The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) cost of each transfer, multiplied by the ring’s round count and traffic factor, then decides when that collective outlasts the step’s computation. At that point the network becomes the pacing constraint for the entire job.

↪ The ring AllReduce derivation, and the schedules that compete with it, are developed in Ring AllReduce.

The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model quantifies the speed of a single link, but the 175B-parameter model requires 1,024 GPUs to work in concert. Scaling these transport primitives from a pair of nodes to a warehouse-scale supercomputer demands a network topology: a specific pattern of connections that maximizes \(\BisectionBandwidth\) while minimizing the hop count and cabling cost for global collective operations.

Self-Check: Question

  1. How does GPUDirect RDMA optimize the end-to-end data path during a multi-node AllReduce gradient exchange compared to traditional host-staged network transfers?

    1. It allows the network interface card (NIC) to read and write GPU High Bandwidth Memory directly over the PCIe bus without staging data through host CPU system RAM
    2. It compresses floating-point tensors in GPU L2 cache before writing them directly to the optical switch buffer
    3. It enables the GPU to execute TCP/IP checksum offloading in CUDA streaming multiprocessors
    4. It re-routes inter-node gradient traffic over host SATA storage buses to bypass network switch congestion

  2. An engineer observes that upgrading network links from \(200\text{ Gb/s}\) (HDR) to \(400\text{ Gb/s}\) (NDR) reduces the transfer time of a \(10\text{ KB}\) pipeline control message by only about \(11\%\), whereas the same upgrade halves the transfer time of a \(350\text{ MB}\) gradient tensor. How does the \(\alpha\)-\(\beta\) performance model explain this discrepancy?

    1. Switch routing tables drop small packets more frequently than large packets due to flowlet timeouts
    2. High-bandwidth optical transceivers disable forward error correction for messages larger than \(1\text{ MB}\)
    3. PCIe bus arbitration introduces dynamic power throttling on transfers smaller than \(64\text{ KB}\)
    4. The \(10\text{ KB}\) message falls below the crossover point (\(n^* = \alpha\beta \approx 75\text{ KB}\)), where fixed startup latency \(\alpha\) dominates, while the \(350\text{ MB}\) message is in the bandwidth-dominated regime (\(n \gg n^*\))

  3. Given an InfiniBand fabric with startup latency \(\alpha = 1.5\,\mu\text{s}\) and bandwidth \(\beta = 50\text{ GB/s}\), calculate the crossover message size \(n^*\). Explain what architectural changes can reduce transfer time for messages below \(n^*\).

  4. On an RNIC that discards out-of-order arrivals rather than retaining them, ______ recovery retransmits from the missing sequence point after packet loss.

  5. True or False: InfiniBand and RoCEv2 achieve losslessness through identical mechanisms because both protocols expose the standard user-space Verbs API to the application.

See Answers →

3.5 Level 3 Switch and Topology

A cluster can have thousands of fast links at the edge and still starve its AllReduce operations if the cross-sectional capacity at the narrowest point in the switching hierarchy is insufficient. The physical arrangement of switches determines whether that cross-section throttles collective communication or sustains full line-rate exchange across all participating workers. Two properties govern this capability: bisection bandwidth \(\BisectionBandwidth\) and whether the fabric is non-blocking, a constraint captured by the bisection bandwidth theorem (principle 6). Bisection bandwidth quantifies the worst-case throughput ceiling that the topology imposes on global collectives.

Definition 3.4: Bisection bandwidth

Bisection bandwidth is a network topology metric defined as the minimum aggregate link capacity crossing any partition that divides the cluster into two equal halves, representing the worst-case throughput ceiling for traffic that must cross that partition, including global collectives.

  1. Significance: Bisection bandwidth \(\BisectionBandwidth\) directly sets the cluster-scale bandwidth ceiling for global synchronization. A 1,024-GPU fat-tree with 400 Gb/s links at 1:1 subscription provides \(\text{BW}_{\text{bisect}} = 512 \times 50\,\text{GB/s} = 25.6\,\text{TB/s}\) per direction; a 4:1 oversubscribed spine reduces this to 6.4 TB/s, increasing the transfer-time lower bound for a fixed cut-crossing payload by 4×. Actual AllReduce slowdown depends on rank placement, the collective schedule, and which links are saturated.
  2. Distinction: Unlike aggregate bandwidth (the sum of all edge link speeds, which can be high even in a poorly connected topology), \(\BisectionBandwidth\) measures global connectivity. A star topology with 1,000 edge links all meeting at one central switch has high aggregate bandwidth but \(\BisectionBandwidth\) limited by that switch’s backplane capacity.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that adding more leaf switches always increases \(\BisectionBandwidth\). In a three-tier fat-tree, oversubscribing the spine layer (giving each spine switch fewer uplinks to the core than downlinks to its leaves) reduces \(\BisectionBandwidth\) below the edge-link total regardless of how many leaf switches are present.

ML training includes global collectives and AllToAll phases that can drive traffic across topology partitions, making full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) a useful capacity target. A smaller cut becomes a bottleneck when the workload’s crossing traffic exceeds what it can deliver within the communication budget, and rank placement together with the collective schedule determines how much traffic crosses that cut.

Bisection bandwidth measures cut capacity; a non-blocking fabric provides paths that can sustain simultaneous input-output pairings at line rate with suitable routing. Full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) and balanced uplink capacity support that goal, but routing must also spread traffic across the available paths. In the oversubscribed designs considered here, reducing a cut’s capacity reduces its effective \(\BisectionBandwidth\), increasing the transfer-time bound for a fixed payload that saturates it, whereas a collective that keeps most traffic within each half need not experience the same slowdown.

↪ A slower cut lengthens the training step only when the extra transfer time cannot hide behind computation. The communication-to-compute diagnostic that tests this is developed in Compute \cap communication.

Definition 3.5: Non-blocking fabric

Non-blocking fabric is a network with sufficient internal path capacity to support any permutation of input-output port pairs simultaneously at full line rate under a suitable routing assignment.

  1. Significance: In ML fleets, a non-blocking fabric can support full-rate permutation traffic with suitable routing, preserving the available \(\BisectionBandwidth\) term for global collectives. A 2:1 oversubscribed spine halves the effective \(\BisectionBandwidth\), doubling the communication time when that cut is the saturated bottleneck and dropping scaling efficiency \(\ScalingEfficiency\) accordingly. In a scenario workload assumed to spend 30 percent of each step communicating, doubling that share while the computation share stays fixed lengthens each step, and the longer step costs roughly 23.1 percent of total cluster throughput. This definition uses 2:1, a common data-center compromise, whereas the bisection definition above and notebook 3.2 use 4:1, a cost-optimized spine; the notebook writes out the throughput formula behind both results.
  2. Distinction: The guarantee concerns a permutation, in which each input and output belongs to one pairing. It does not promise full line rate from every sender to every receiver at once, nor does it make an arbitrary routing assignment free of contention.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that non-blocking means zero congestion. Endpoint congestion (incast) can still occur if multiple senders simultaneously target the same receiver port, regardless of how much internal fabric capacity is available.

Fat-trees build on Clos-style non-blocking network principles to supply full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) (Clos 1953), rail-optimized networks trade some global routing flexibility for lower cabling cost, and dragonfly topologies reduce optical link counts while introducing workload-placement constraints (Kim et al. 2008). This trade-off between guaranteed bisection bandwidth and cabling cost drives every topology decision in large-scale ML clusters, as the comparisons in section 3.5.2, section 3.5.3, and section 3.5.4 show, and every switched design begins at the first-hop switch that aggregates a rack or rail of servers.

3.5.1 Top-of-rack (ToR) and the failure domain

Consider a rack containing four nodes with eight GPUs each: if all 32 accelerators attach to a single top-of-rack (ToR) switch with no alternate path, losing that switch disconnects the entire rack from the job at once. Because the ToR aggregates every server link in the rack, it becomes a shared failure domain, one level of the physical hierarchy defined in Node health and reliability, and recovery then depends on available paths, surviving state, and the job’s recovery policy. This one-switch-per-rack wiring is the simpler baseline used here to teach radix and failure domains. The reference cluster itself wires each GPU to a rail switch, a layout developed in section 3.5.3. That layout reshapes the failure domain, because losing one rail switch cuts one GPU in each of many nodes rather than every GPU in one rack.

The switch radix,5 its number of ports, determines how many endpoints and uplinks it can connect. In the reference allocation, a 64-port switch assigns 32 ports to servers, one for each of 32 GPUs, and 32 ports to the spine. With equal port rates, the 1:1 ratio avoids oversubscription at that boundary; delivered throughput still depends on forwarding and the paths beyond it. The 1,024 GPUs-GPU cluster therefore requires approximately 32 such endpoint groups, although the physical rack count also depends on node density and the cabling layout.

5 Switch radix: High-end switches such as NVIDIA Quantum-2 offer a radix of 64 ports, each at 400 Gb/s. As model scale pushes toward 100,000 GPUs, higher radix (128 or 256 ports), larger local accelerator domains, or accepted topology constraints are the main ways to avoid adding switch tiers, each of which adds switch latency and cost.

The same boundary shapes state placement. To survive a rack-wide outage, a job needs recoverable state outside that rack and a way to resume on available capacity. Topology-aware scheduling must therefore consider both fast communication paths and the failures that several nearby resources share.

↪ Nested failure domains, and why redundant state must span them to survive a correlated rack or ToR outage, are developed in Correlated failures.

3.5.2 Fat-tree (Clos) networks

The ToR provides non-blocking bandwidth within a single rack, but connecting the reference cluster’s 32 endpoint groups into a cluster that preserves full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) across every possible communication pair requires a topology that scales cross-sectional capacity with cluster size. The fat-tree (also called a Clos network) achieves this by adding parallel spine switches at each tier, so the aggregate uplink capacity always matches the total edge bandwidth feeding into it. In this vocabulary, leaf switches attach servers or racks, spine switches connect leaves within a pod, and core switches connect multiple pods when a third tier is needed; other sources call the leaf and spine tiers edge and aggregation. A pod here is a switching domain, the group of leaves that one set of spines joins, which can be narrower than the multi-rack pod of The Pod. A multi-rack pod in that sense can contain several fat-tree pods.

Definition 3.6: Fat-tree

Fat-tree is a hierarchical ML cluster network topology in which the number of parallel paths (and therefore aggregate cross-sectional capacity) increases at each switch tier toward the spine, providing full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) and multiple equal-cost routes between any two nodes (Al-Fares et al. 2008).

  1. Significance: A \(\SwitchRadix\)-ary fat-tree built from radix-\(\SwitchRadix\) switches supports \(\SwitchRadix^2/2\) hosts in a standalone two-tier configuration and \(\SwitchRadix^3/4\) hosts in a three-tier configuration with full \(\BisectionBandwidth\). With 64-port switches, a standalone two-tier fabric supports 2,048 GPUs and a three-tier fabric supports 65,536 hosts. This is a capacity bound for suitable routing and admissible traffic; it does not eliminate endpoint contention or guarantee that a particular AllReduce implementation reaches line rate.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a standard tree (where bandwidth at the root is a single bottleneck shared by all leaves), a fat-tree replaces each root with multiple spine switches whose combined uplink capacity matches the total edge bandwidth, eliminating the bottleneck.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that fat-trees guarantee zero network cost. They require \(\mathcal{O}(\TopologyEndpointCount\log \TopologyEndpointCount)\) switches and dense cabling, so a non-blocking three-tier fat-tree at the 4,000-GPU scale needs hundreds of switches and more than ten thousand links before a single accelerator is purchased.
Al-Fares, Mohammad, Alexander Loukissas, and Amin Vahdat. 2008. “A Scalable, Commodity Data Center Network Architecture.” ACM SIGCOMM Computer Communication Review 38 (4): 63–74. https://doi.org/10.1145/1402946.1402967.

A three-tier radix-4 fabric (\(\SwitchRadix=4\)) shows the non-blocking structure at a scale small enough to draw (figure 9), with four core switches interconnecting four dashed pod modules. Leaf and spine switches split their ports between the tiers below and above; core switches use all their ports to connect pods. Each tier boundary therefore carries 16 links. The highlighted route traverses five switches and is one of \((\SwitchRadix/2)^2 = 4\) equal-cost routes from a GPU in Pod 1 to Pod 4. Parallel paths supply the capacity, while routing must distribute traffic across them to use it.

The fat-tree6 is a common default for ML clusters because a non-blocking design can provide full \(\BisectionBandwidth\) across arbitrary partitions. This capacity benefits global AllReduce even though implementations such as rings and trees do not require every pair of ranks to communicate at once. The number of hosts that the guarantee can cover depends on switch radix and tier count.

6 Fat-tree (Clos network): The underlying multi-stage switching theory was invented by Clos (1953) at Bell Labs to minimize the number of electromechanical crosspoints in telephone exchanges. Leiserson (1985) at MIT generalized the concept as the “fat-tree,” where the tree is “fat” because link bandwidth increases toward the root, proving it could emulate any network of equal hardware volume. The same cost-minimization logic that drove 1950s telephony now drives ML cluster design: minimize switch count while guaranteeing non-blocking connectivity for global AllReduce.

Clos, C. 1953. “A Study of Non-Blocking Switching Networks.” Bell System Technical Journal 32 (2): 406–24. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1953.tb01433.x.
Leiserson, Charles E. 1985. “Fat-Trees: Universal Networks for Hardware-Efficient Supercomputing.” IEEE Transactions on Computers C-34 (10): 892–901. https://doi.org/10.1109/tc.1985.6312192.

A fat-tree built from switches with radix \(\SwitchRadix\) supports \(\FatTreeHostCount= \SwitchRadix^{3}/4\) hosts at three tiers. A standalone two-tier non-blocking fabric can instead use \(\SwitchRadix\) leaves, each with \(\SwitchRadix/2\) host ports and \(\SwitchRadix/2\) uplinks, connected to \(\SwitchRadix/2\) spines. Each spine uses all \(\SwitchRadix\) ports to reach the leaves, providing \(\SwitchRadix^{2}/2 =\) 2,048 host ports for radix-64 switches. In the three-tier design, each spine switch reserves half of its ports for the core tier and uses the other \(\SwitchRadix/2\) ports to connect to leaves, so one pod has \(\SwitchRadix/2\) leaves and \((\SwitchRadix/2)^2 =\) 1,024 host ports. Because each radix-\(\SwitchRadix\) core switch connects one port to each pod, the core tier can join up to \(\SwitchRadix\) pods for the full \(\SwitchRadix \times (\SwitchRadix/2)^2 = \SwitchRadix^3/4\) host capacity. These are different port allocations and switch counts rather than two ways of counting the same fabric, and while the 1,024-GPU reference cluster fits within a standalone two-tier design, the distinction matters as soon as a cluster outgrows that limit. A 4,096-GPU deployment cannot join two fully populated standalone 2,048-host fabrics at their spines, because every spine port is already occupied by a leaf connection. Instead, it is built as four 1,024-host pods of the three-tier design, joined through a core tier. A concrete bisection estimate shows how quickly oversubscription at that spine or core boundary turns into synchronization delay.

Napkin Math 3.2: Bisection bandwidth: The cost of oversubscription
Problem: A cluster designer is choosing between a “non-blocking” (1:1) fat-tree and a “cost-optimized” (4:1) spine for a $300M cluster of 1024 GPUs. This is a separate economics scenario rather than the running 175B job: each GPU contributes a 5 GB gradient payload, and the workload is assumed to spend \(\CommunicationTimeFraction=\) 30 percent of each step communicating. That share is a scenario input. It is larger than the 13 percent of the illustrative schedule in figure 2 and far smaller than the exposed share in the introduction’s GPT-3 synchronization example (The energy accounting boundary), which assumes pure data parallelism with no overlap; none of the three is a measured share of the reference job. How much slower will the AllReduce be on the cheaper network, and what does that cost?

Math: Bisection bandwidth (\(\BisectionBandwidth\)) is the minimum pipe diameter between halves of the cluster. Assume a unidirectional ring whose ranks alternate between the two halves of the cluster. Every ring edge then crosses the cut, with \(\AcceleratorCount/2\) senders in each direction. Assume balanced routing, no competing traffic, and negligible startup latency. ReduceScatter plus AllGather contributes a traffic factor \(2(\AcceleratorCount-1)/\AcceleratorCount\) = 1.998 per GPU; multiplying by \(\AcceleratorCount/2\) gives the bytes crossing the cut in each direction. A ring that groups ranks within each half would have fewer crossing edges and a different cut-load bound.

  1. Non-blocking (1:1): \(\BisectionBandwidth =\) 512 \(\times\) 50 GB/s \(=\) 25,600 GB/s per direction.
    • Time: 5,115 GB / 25,600 GB/s \(\approx\) 0.200 s.
  2. Oversubscribed (4:1): \(\BisectionBandwidth =\) 25,600 GB/s divided by 4 = 6,400 GB/s per direction.
    • Time: 5,115 GB / 6,400 GB/s \(\approx\) 0.799 s.
  3. Throughput: If the communication share stretches by the slowdown factor \(\CommunicationSlowdownFactor\) while computation stays fixed, then \(\text{relative throughput} = \frac{1}{(1-\CommunicationTimeFraction) + \CommunicationTimeFraction\,\CommunicationSlowdownFactor} = \frac{1}{0.70 + 0.30 \times 4} = \frac{1}{1.90} \approx 0.53\), a loss of about 47.4 percent of cluster throughput.
  4. Cost: That loss times the $300M capital cost is about $142.1M.

Systems insight: For this cut-saturating placement, saving money on core switches creates a 4× bottleneck for global synchronization, and on a $300M cluster that bottleneck idles about $142.1M of compute. The same formula gives the 23.1 percent loss of the 2:1 spine in the non-blocking definition. This is an equivalent-capacity cost under the stated workload assumptions, not a direct equipment loss. Oversubscription must be evaluated against the collective traffic actually crossing the constrained cut.

The subscription ratio at each tier therefore determines whether a fat-tree can sustain global collectives, and the tier count determines how far it can grow. Scaling beyond the 2,048-host two-tier limit requires a three-tier architecture with core switches, enabling the fabric to reach 65,536 hosts, but the added scale carries both cost and latency. Each of the \(\SwitchRadix\) pods contains \(\SwitchRadix/2\) leaf switches and \(\SwitchRadix/2\) spine switches (\(\SwitchRadix^2\) switches across all pods), and their \(\SwitchRadix/2\) spines each send \(\SwitchRadix/2\) uplinks into \((\SwitchRadix/2)^2 = \SwitchRadix^2/4\) core switches. A non-blocking \(\SwitchRadix=64\) three-tier tree therefore requires \(5\SwitchRadix^2/4 =\) 5,120 switches across the core, spine, and leaf layers. At $10,000 to $50,000 per switch, the switching layer alone represents $51M to $256M. Typical hop counts also rise, from two inter-switch links and three switch traversals for intra-pod traffic (leaf-spine-leaf) to four inter-switch links and five switch traversals for inter-pod traffic (leaf-spine-core-spine-leaf), adding serialization delay and switch processing time to the \(\MessageStartupLatency\) latency term across thousands of synchronization steps. Rail-optimized topologies respond to both pressures by matching the physical cabling pattern to the collective communication pattern.

Checkpoint 3.2: Fat-tree topologies

These questions check whether hierarchical switch-fabric trade-offs are clear:

3.5.3 Rail-optimized topology

When tensor-parallel groups stay inside each node’s high-bandwidth NVSwitch domain, data parallelism exchanges gradients only between GPUs that hold the same model shard in the same local slot position across nodes, yet a standard fat-tree wires all eight GPUs of a node into a shared ToR switch. A rail-optimized topology instead cables external network ports by local slot position (figure 10): GPU 0 in every node connects to the Rail 0 switch, GPU 1 connects to Rail 1, and so on across the cluster.

This slot-aligned wiring exploits the stratified traffic that 3D parallelism produces. Because each node holds a tensor-parallel group, each data-parallel replica assigns the corresponding model shard to the same local GPU slot (where the slot number is a local rank within a node, distinct from the worker’s global rank in the distributed job). During data-parallel AllReduce, GPU 0 on one node synchronizes its shard’s gradients with GPU 0 on other nodes, but rarely communicates with GPU 1. Isolating each local rank onto its own rail switch fabric removes same-rank traffic from shared rack switches and, when a rail fits on a single switch, shortens the path between corresponding GPUs. A simple hop-count estimate shows the payoff.

Napkin Math 3.3: The rail-optimized dividend
Problem: A team is synchronizing per-rank data-parallel gradients across 128 nodes. In a standard fat-tree, each message between same-rank GPUs traverses its source leaf switch, a spine switch, and the destination leaf switch (3 switch traversals). In a rail-optimized network built from rail switches with at least one port per node, all corresponding GPUs sit on the same rail switch (one traversal). On the reference cluster that requires rail switches with more ports than the radix-64 switch used so far, so the estimate describes a higher-radix design. How much latency dividend does the rail design earn when a rail fits on one switch?

Math: This estimate counts only the switch-traversal contribution to communication latency (\(\MessageStartupLatency\)), using the same assumed delay for each traversal.

  1. Standard latency: 3 traversals \(\times\) 0.6 μs = 1.8 μs.
  2. Rail-optimized: 1 traversal \(\times\) 0.6 μs = 0.6 μs.
  3. Result: 3× lower latency.

Systems insight: For synchronous data-parallel AllReduce, this 3× reduction applies to the modeled switch-latency contribution, not to total collective time. Its effect on scaling efficiency (\(\ScalingEfficiency\)) depends on payload size, delivered bandwidth, compute time, and overlap. The single-switch rail in this example avoids the spine traversal for same-rank traffic. The dividend is conditional. On the reference cluster’s radix-64 switches each rail outgrows one switch and regains the extra traversals, so what rails reliably buy there is isolation, developed below.

The hop saving is only part of the case for rails. Large language model fleets adopt the pattern because it matches how their parallelism plan divides traffic.

Lighthouse 3.1: Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3): The rail-optimized fleet
Archetype A’s use of 3D parallelism generates two distinct traffic patterns that pull in opposite directions: (1) large, bandwidth-hungry gradient averaging for data parallelism, and (2) high-frequency, latency-sensitive activation exchanges for tensor parallelism. Keeping tensor parallelism inside each node’s NVLink domain leaves data-parallel gradient traffic on the fabric, and rails give each same-rank data-parallel group its own switch network. A rail can provide a single switch hop between nodes when its endpoint count fits the available switch ports. Larger rails require additional switching stages, so rail alignment alone does not guarantee single-hop communication. Either way, the lesson for Archetype A is that how the fabric is wired to the parallelism plan matters as much as how many accelerators it connects.

For the cluster of 1,024 GPUs spanning 128 nodes, this creates 8 parallel networks of 128 GPUs each, requiring at least one switch port per endpoint in each rail. A single-switch realization therefore needs a radix at least as large as the rail endpoint count; the earlier radix-64 switch cannot realize this rail alone, which is why notebook 3.3 assumed higher-radix rail switches. Built from radix-64 switches instead, each rail becomes a small two-tier fabric that preserves rank alignment but adds hops.
