For the bandwidth-hungry gradient exchanges of data parallelism, the larger benefit is isolation: each rail carries only its own rank’s traffic, so the eight rail networks operate concurrently without competing for shared spine capacity. When a rail fits on one switch, the shorter path also trims the startup cost a ring pays on every round. That same isolation creates a sharp trade-off, because traffic that must reach GPUs of different ranks (such as expert routing in mixture of experts (MoE), or pipeline-stage handoffs that do not align with the rail wiring) requires bridging across rails. Large clusters therefore often employ a hybrid approach, using rail-optimized leaves for same-rank data-parallel traffic while bridging the rails with a full fat-tree spine to support cross-rank communication patterns.

Checkpoint 3.3: Rail-optimized networks

These questions check whether workload-specific network-design trade-offs are clear:

3.5.4 Dragonfly and torus alternatives

At tens of thousands of endpoints, a non-blocking three-tier fat-tree forces a third layer of core switches and tens of thousands of long optical cables into the budget, driving network capital expenditure into the tens or hundreds of millions of dollars. Two alternative topologies attack that cost wall from opposite directions: tapering the global switch tier or removing external fabric switches altogether. A dragonfly7 topology organizes high-radix routers (the dragonfly literature’s name for switches) into fully connected groups joined by global optical links. Each group has \(\DragonflyGroupRouterCount\) routers, and each router has \(\DragonflyTerminalPortCount\) terminal ports and \(\DragonflyGlobalChannelCount\) global channels. The ratio of \(\DragonflyGlobalChannelCount\) to \(\DragonflyTerminalPortCount\) sets how much global bandwidth each group receives relative to the traffic its terminals can inject, so global bandwidth is a design parameter rather than a fixed percentage of aggregate injection bandwidth. The balanced configuration analyzed by Kim et al. (2008), \(\DragonflyGroupRouterCount=2\DragonflyTerminalPortCount=2\DragonflyGlobalChannelCount\), is one setting of that knob. The routers in a group are fully connected, and the groups are joined by global links. A minimal route between two groups therefore takes at most one local hop to the router holding the right global link, one global hop, and one local hop in the destination group. No minimal path exceeds three router-to-router hops, regardless of system size. Short paths come cheaply, but performance for jobs spanning groups depends on the chosen \(\DragonflyTerminalPortCount/\DragonflyGlobalChannelCount\) ratio, routing, and traffic matrix, because every inter-group transfer shares the provisioned global links.

7 Dragonfly topology: Introduced by Kim et al. (2008) at ISCA 2008, the dragonfly uses high-radix routers grouped into fully connected “super-nodes.” For systems with at least 16,000 terminals, the architecture achieves a 52 percent network-cost reduction relative to a folded Clos (the fat-tree of section 3.5.2) of equivalent bandwidth, though global optical link oversubscription can create AllToAll bottlenecks during MoE token routing.

Kim, J., W. J. Dally, S. Scott, and D. Abts. 2008. “Technology-Driven, Highly-Scalable Dragonfly Topology.” 2008 International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 77–88. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca.2008.19.

A torus topology connects each node directly to its neighbors in a multidimensional grid, most commonly a 3D torus where every node links to its six adjacent peers (up/down, left/right, front/back). The design offers full local bandwidth with minimal switching hardware, because every neighbor is a single hop away. However, global communication requires traversing the diameter of the mesh (\(\mathcal{O}(\TopologyEndpointCount^{1/3})\) hops), making latency scale poorly with cluster size. Google adopted this architecture for its Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) pods because the collectives that dominate its training workloads can be scheduled as rings along each torus dimension, a nearest-neighbor pattern that maps naturally onto the physical grid. The limitation becomes apparent with MoE, which relies on AllToAll communication patterns. On a torus, these random permutations can congest the limited \(\BisectionBandwidth\) of the mesh and degrade performance relative to a non-blocking switch fabric.

The trade-off becomes concrete for the 4,096-endpoint deployment that the three-tier fat-tree reached with four pods. Arranged as a \(16 \times 16 \times 16\) torus with wraparound links, the same endpoints need no external switching stages. Splitting the cube in half across one axis cuts a \(16 \times 16\) plane of interior links and an equal plane of wraparound links, so only \(2 \times 16^2 =\) 512 links cross the bisection, against 2,048 in the non-blocking fat-tree. With 50 GB/s per link and direction, the torus offers 25.6 TB/s across the cut against the fat-tree’s 102.4 TB/s, a 4× gap. Because wraparound links cap the distance along each 16-node axis at 8 hops, the torus’s worst-case path across all three axes crosses \(3 \times 8 =\) 24 neighbor hops against the fat-tree’s five switch traversals. Because a cubic torus’s cut grows only as \(\TopologyEndpointCount^{2/3}\) while its endpoint count grows as \(\TopologyEndpointCount\), that capacity gap widens at fixed link rate. Whether the gap matters depends on the traffic. A ring AllReduce scheduled along each torus dimension moves data only between neighbors and combines contributions before forwarding them, so it leans little on the narrow cut. An MoE AllToAll or an embedding exchange instead sends a large share of its traffic between distant endpoints and drives it straight into the cut, with the exact pressure set by placement and message sizes. Consequently, the collective schedule and traffic matrix determine whether the torus’s lower cross-sectional capacity limits completion time.

↪ AllToAll schedules and their cost at scale, where every rank sends a distinct share to every other rank, are developed in AllToAll Exchanges at Scale.

At the scale of the 1,024-GPU reference cluster, the four families range in cut capacity from 25.6 TB/s in the non-blocking fat-tree down to 6.4 TB/s in a 3D torus, while an assumed interconnect cost runs from $1.5/Gb/s for the switchless torus to $4/Gb/s for the tapered dragonfly (figure 11). The cheapest capacity is also the least capacity, which is the trade every topology decision prices. The dragonfly’s assumed cost per Gb/s is the highest here because its savings come from replacing long optical runs and switch tiers at large scale, which Kim et al. (2008) reports for systems of at least 16,000 terminals; at 1,024 GPUs it has few such runs to replace. Radix, endpoint count, link speed, routing mechanisms, and equipment pricing all shift these trade-offs; the topology family alone dictates neither raw cross-sectional capacity nor operational capital cost.

3.5.5 Optical circuit switching and reconfigurable topology

Every topology compared in figure 11 is fixed when the facility is cabled, locking the cluster into a single physical graph regardless of which workload runs on it. A statically cabled Clos hierarchy (section 3.5.2) fixes the oversubscription ratio and bisection pattern whether the active job is pipeline-dominated, data-parallel, or AllToAll expert-routed. A statically cabled torus (section 3.5.4) is equally rigid: it fits dimension-ordered rings, but it congests under MoE AllToAll traffic and cannot rewire its links to match the job.

Switching those multi-tier fabrics electrically also extracts a recurring power tax at every hop. Each electrical packet switch inspects headers and chooses an outgoing link only after an Optical-Electrical-Optical (OEO) conversion: incoming photons hit a photodiode, high-speed SerDes circuits deserialize the electrical signal, switch ASICs buffer and route packets across crossbars, and output SerDes drive lasers to re-transmit light over optical fiber. At 800 Gb/s and 1.6 Tb/s per-port data rates, SerDes and transceiver electronics consume significant energy (section 3.3.3), and every additional switch tier repeats that conversion cost for each bit that crosses it. Changing the physical connections between node groups in light, for the duration of a workload allocation rather than packet by packet, avoids both static wiring and per-hop OEO conversion.

Definition 3.7: Optical circuit switching

Optical circuit switching (OCS) is a physical networking architecture that routes optical signals directly using Micro-Electro-Mechanical Systems (MEMS) mirror arrays without Optical-Electrical-Optical (OEO) conversion, enabling dynamic, software-defined topology reconfiguration and high energy efficiency at warehouse scale.

  1. Significance: An optical circuit bypasses packet-by-packet electronic processing in the switching path. The complete system still consumes power for actuators, control, and transceivers. Its compatibility across line rates depends on optical loss, wavelengths, and endpoint budgets, so protocol transparency does not imply unrestricted upgrades.
  2. Distinction: Unlike electrical packet switches (which inspect headers, buffer packets in SRAM, and make nanosecond routing decisions per packet), an OCS establishes semi-static physical optical circuits with millisecond-scale switching latency (\(10\)–\(30\) ms), making it suitable for topology-level reconfiguration rather than dynamic packet routing.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming OCS replaces packet switching entirely. OCS operates at circuit granularity: it configures cluster-scale topologies (such as 3D tori or job-specific sub-clusters) and routes around hardware faults, while packet switching or accelerator-native interconnects handle fine-grained, intra-step message routing.

Google’s TPU v4 supercomputer (Jouppi et al. 2023) deployed optical circuit switching in production. Instead of wiring 4,096 chips into an inflexible electrical fat-tree, the system groups accelerators into \(4{\times}4{\times}4\) node cubes and routes the inter-cube links through an array of 3D MEMS optical circuit switches (codenamed Palomar).

Jouppi, Norm, George Kurian, Sheng Li, Peter Ma, Rahul Nagarajan, Lifeng Nai, Nishant Patil, et al. 2023. “TPU v4: An Optically Reconfigurable Supercomputer for Machine Learning with Hardware Support for Embeddings.” Proceedings of the 50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1–14. https://doi.org/10.1145/3579371.3589350.

The optical layer serves two purposes, adapting the topology to the workload and recovering from faults. For workload adaptation, the physical interconnect can be reconfigured to match the communication structure of the running model. A training run using 1D pipeline parallelism can configure its slice as an elongated cylinder to streamline stage-to-stage transfers. Conversely, a 2D or 3D tensor-parallel partition can configure a compact cubic 3D torus that minimizes average hop count, while an MoE workload can maximize bisection bandwidth across specific expert groups.

Optical switching also supports fault isolation by changing connections around unavailable hardware where spare connectivity permits. The capability matters because in a statically cabled torus, a single failed link or node can break a ring along an entire dimension and degrade every job that spans it. With optical circuit switching, the mirrors can be redirected to bypass the failed cube or fiber and splice healthy cubes into a complete, smaller torus slice without manual recabling. The fabric manager must first detect the fault, choose a feasible allocation, and establish the new circuits, which means circuit-switching time is only one part of recovery; a training job may also need to rebuild its process groups and restore state before computation resumes, so optical reconfiguration latency alone does not specify recovery time.

The trade-off is switching latency. Because MEMS mirrors physically rotate to steer collimated light beams, reconfiguration occurs on a mechanical timescale that is much longer than packet forwarding in an established electrical path. Circuit setup changes connectivity, while packet forwarding uses connectivity already in place. Consequently, OCS raises the power and protocol ceiling of the switching layer while leaving reconfiguration latency as the binding constraint, so it is deployed for job-level topology allocation and fault isolation, not for per-message dynamic routing. Within that limit, OCS turns the choice of topology from a decision fixed when the fabric is cabled into one made for each job.

Each topology comparison so far has considered one traffic pattern at a time. In practice, clusters serve multiple workloads with different communication patterns, and topology selection must balance their competing demands.

Checkpoint 3.4: Topology selection for your workload

The choice of network topology dictates the upper bound of training efficiency. Warm up by matching each single-workload pattern to its ideal topology, then design a fabric that must serve several at once.

Single-workload picks

Designing for a mixed cluster

You are designing the network for a new ML cluster that will run two primary workloads: (1) training a 175B-parameter language model using 3D parallelism (tensor, pipeline, and data parallelism), and (2) serving a mixture-of-experts model that relies heavily on AllToAll communication to route tokens to the correct experts.

Topology provides the structural capacity for the cluster of 1,024 GPUs to communicate, but structure alone does not guarantee performance. When the 1,024 GPUs synchronize contributions to the model’s 350 GB gradient state, that theoretical capacity collides with the reality of fabric behavior: congestion control and routing dynamics determine whether this traffic flows smoothly or gridlocks under the strict synchronization of the BSP model.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A cluster designer builds a three-tier non-blocking fat-tree network using radix-64 switches (\(k = 64\)). What is the maximum number of hosts this fabric can support at 1:1 bisection bandwidth subscription, and how many total switches are required across all tiers?

    1. \(2{,}048\) hosts and \(128\) switches
    2. \(16{,}384\) hosts and \(1{,}024\) switches
    3. \(65{,}536\) hosts and \(5{,}120\) switches
    4. \(262{,}144\) hosts and \(20{,}480\) switches

  2. Why does a rail-optimized network topology significantly reduce synchronization latency for 3D-parallel training jobs compared to a standard uniform fat-tree?

    1. It replaces optical fiber cables with liquid-cooled copper buses across all rows
    2. It physically connects all GPUs of the same local rank (e.g., all GPU 0s) across server nodes to dedicated rail switches, allowing same-rail transfers within a leaf group to use a single switch hop
    3. It eliminates the need for intra-node NVLink by routing all tensor-parallel traffic through Top-of-Rack switches
    4. It dynamically routes all AllReduce packets through the GPU display engine to bypass PCIe contention

  3. A cluster operator considers saving capital expenditure by building a \(4:1\) oversubscribed spine in a 1,024-GPU training cluster. Explain the quantitative and economic impact of this design on a 100 GB per GPU AllReduce collective during distributed training.

  4. Why did Google adopt a 3D torus topology for its TPU Pods, and what communication workload exposes the primary structural limitation of this topology?

    1. The 3D torus minimizes cabling cost and external switch hardware by leveraging direct neighbor-to-neighbor links that suit structured transformer collectives, but suffers high hop count (\(\mathcal{O}(N^{1/3})\)) and bisection congestion under irregular AllToAll traffic from Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models
    2. The 3D torus provides full non-blocking bisection bandwidth for random permutation traffic, but fails when running ring AllReduce due to cyclic buffer dependencies
    3. The 3D torus eliminates Forward Error Correction requirements, but cannot support FP16 numerical precision
    4. The 3D torus reduces power consumption by disabling physical link SerDes, but restricts cluster scale to 32 nodes

  5. True or False: In a data center network, aggregate bandwidth (the sum of all endpoint link rates) and bisection bandwidth (\(\text{BW}_{\text{bisect}}\)) are identical metrics.

See Answers →

3.6 Level 4 Fabric Behavior, Congestion, and Routing

A collective can finish late even when the topology provides full \(\BisectionBandwidth\). Levels 1 through 3 determine how long a transfer takes on an unloaded path; fabric behavior determines how much longer it takes once the job’s own synchronized traffic loads that path. Competing flows can concentrate on one output path, and the BSP barrier introduced in section 3.2 exposes any delay that extends the last required transfer. The relevant paths are those used by the participating workers; a congested link elsewhere in the fleet need not affect the job. In the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), measured transfer durations contribute to \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), while residual waiting at synchronization contributes to \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\), the point where the communication axis of C³ hands its cost to the coordination axis. A timeline must assign each interval consistently so the same congestion delay is not counted twice.

The p99 tail latency introduced with the BSP barrier helps expose delays that an average hides, but a particular barrier waits for its last required participant, not for a percentile. Evaluating a fabric therefore requires both transfer-time distributions and the collective completion times they produce under load.

3.6.1 Priority flow control (PFC)

The BSP weakest-link property means that even a single dropped packet can trigger a retransmission timeout that stalls the global barrier for milliseconds, a delay that can rival the entire communication phase of a training step. PFC-based RoCEv2 fabrics prevent the drop in the first place by running PFC, the pause mechanism introduced with the RDMA stacks (section 3.4.2), in lossless mode, so that upstream senders stop before any buffer saturates (Guo et al. 2016; Gangidi et al. 2024).

Definition 3.8: Priority flow control

Priority flow control (PFC) is a link-layer mechanism used by RoCEv2 ML cluster fabrics to prevent switch buffer overflow. When a port’s queue depth crosses a configured threshold, the switch sends PAUSE frames to an upstream sender, which throttles injection on a per-priority basis without dropping packets.

  1. Significance: In PFC-based RoCEv2 deployments, PFC is the foundation for a lossless Ethernet service. A PFC PAUSE frame must reach the upstream sender before the buffer overflows, so each pause threshold must leave headroom for the data still in flight during roughly one link round trip.
  2. Distinction: Unlike standard IEEE 802.3 flow control, which pauses all traffic classes on a link, PFC operates per priority class, allowing latency-sensitive control messages to continue flowing while only pausing the congested gradient data queue.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that PFC solves congestion. It transforms packet loss into congestion spreading. PFC itself places no bound on how far PAUSE frames propagate, so a single faulty transceiver can pause traffic far from its own link unless a watchdog intervenes.

The danger of PFC lies in its cascading nature. When a switch port’s buffer fills, it sends a PAUSE frame upstream, which can move congestion into the upstream switch and trigger another PAUSE frame. A slow receiver, routing hot spot, or true fan-in burst can therefore pause links unrelated to the original queue. This behavior, known as congestion spreading, is the primary operational risk of PFC-based lossless Ethernet, and the flows it stalls without ever touching the congested queue are called victim flows. Fan-in is one trigger, but it is not intrinsic to every collective. A bandwidth-optimal ring exchanges data point to point, although multiple ring flows can still collide at a shared egress or an oversubscribed cut and create a hot spot without a single aggregation receiver (section 3.6.5).

PAUSE frames climb back tier by tier and stall senders far from the full port.

3.6.2 Lossless fabric hazards and watchdog recovery

While priority flow control provides the illusion of a lossless physical layer, relying on link-layer backpressure introduces structural failure modes that do not exist in loss-tolerant packet-switched networks. When switch queues cannot drop packets to relieve congestion, pressure propagates backward through the network graph, creating two distinct hyperscale failure modes: buffer-dependency deadlocks and PFC storms.

Definition 3.9: Cyclic buffer dependency deadlock

Cyclic buffer dependency deadlock is a permanent distributed lockup in a lossless network, whether its backpressure uses PFC PAUSE frames or InfiniBand credits. A set of switch port queues forms a closed dependency cycle, and each queue holds packets destined for the next queue while backpressure from that same queue pauses it.

  1. Significance: A single such deadlock permanently halts traffic across all participating ports and upstream links, requiring manual intervention or watchdog timeouts that discard packets to recover. InfiniBand fabrics prevent it through deadlock-free routing and virtual-lane assignment, and PFC-based Ethernet fabrics need the same discipline in their routing and priority configuration.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a PFC storm (which is a transient cascade of backpressure triggered by a slow-draining endpoint), a buffer-dependency deadlock is a static, self-sustaining topological cycle that cannot resolve itself even after traffic injection stops.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming multi-stage Clos (fat-tree) fabrics are inherently immune to deadlocks. Dynamic multipath routing, link failures, and address resolution flooding can introduce cyclic forwarding dependencies across switch tiers.

The graph mechanics of buffer-dependency deadlocks

In a lossless network, a switch cannot forward a packet to downstream buffer \(\PacketBuffer_j\) if \(\PacketBuffer_j\) is full; instead, it holds the packet in upstream buffer \(\PacketBuffer_i\) and awaits credit or pause clearance. A directed buffer dependency graph \(\BufferDependencyGraph= (\BufferDependencyVertexSet, \BufferDependencyEdgeSet)\) formalizes this relationship. Each vertex \(\PacketBuffer_k \in \BufferDependencyVertexSet\) represents a packet buffer or virtual lane queue at a switch port. A directed edge \((\PacketBuffer_i, \PacketBuffer_j) \in \BufferDependencyEdgeSet\) indicates that a packet currently occupying buffer \(\PacketBuffer_i\) requires buffer \(\PacketBuffer_j\) along its routing path to make forward progress.

A directed cycle in the buffer dependency graph is a necessary condition for a cyclic buffer dependency deadlock (definition 3.9), but a cycle alone does not establish that progress has stopped. The cyclic dependency in equation 1 becomes a deadlock when the occupied buffers cannot drain and every participant waits for its successor:

\[\PacketBuffer_1 \to \PacketBuffer_2 \to \dots \to \PacketBuffer_k \to \PacketBuffer_1 \tag{1}\]

When each buffer in the cycle fills beyond its configured pause threshold, each port asserts a PFC PAUSE frame to its predecessor. Because no buffer can advance a packet into a paused successor, no buffer can drain, and no port can de-assert its PAUSE signal. The entire cycle freezes permanently. A four-switch ring whose queues (\(\PacketBuffer_1\) through \(\PacketBuffer_4\)) are completely full shows this mutual protocol interlock (figure 12). Solid gray arrows trace packet forwarding dependencies advancing clockwise (\(\PacketBuffer_1 \to \PacketBuffer_2 \to \PacketBuffer_3 \to \PacketBuffer_4 \to \PacketBuffer_1\)), while dashed red PAUSE frames propagate counterclockwise. Each full queue pauses its upstream predecessor while waiting for its downstream successor to drain, permanently freezing all four queues until an external watchdog intervenes to drop frames.

Routing changes and flooding can create the dependencies needed for deadlock. During link failure or Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) convergence, inconsistent forwarding tables can create transient loops. A lossy IP network eventually discards a looping packet when its time-to-live expires; a lossless class has no such release, so looping traffic can fill the buffers along the loop and hold them. If traffic occupies a cycle of mutually blocked queues, backpressure can prevent progress until recovery breaks the dependency. Multicast or broadcast replication can also fill queues whose dependencies intersect, while cross-connects and asymmetric failover paths can introduce cycles absent from the intended hierarchical route. None of these conditions alone proves a deadlock; the occupied buffers and pause states must form the closed waiting cycle described by the model. Microsoft’s production RoCE deployment encountered the flooding trigger directly (war story 3.1).

War Story 3.1: Microsoft's PFC deadlock (2016)
Context: A team led by Chuanxiong Guo at Microsoft Research deployed RoCEv2 across Microsoft’s data centers, a fleet operating at the scale of tens of thousands of servers, to support latency-sensitive services. Their design provided a lossless fabric with PFC, and the team developed a Differentiated Services Code Point (DSCP)-based PFC mechanism to extend the deployment beyond virtual local area network (VLAN) boundaries (Guo et al. 2016).

Mechanism: PFC interacted with Ethernet flooding to create a cyclic buffer dependency. A dead server with an incomplete mapping between an IP address and a media-access-control address caused upstream switches to flood lossless packets to every port; PFC PAUSE frames then propagated upstream in response to the flooded traffic, forming a closed loop of paused buffers.

Impact: Lossless traffic through the switches in the cycle stopped entirely and stayed stopped, because no buffer in the dependency loop could drain without outside intervention.

Fix: Microsoft blocked broadcast and multicast traffic from entering lossless traffic classes, and dropped lossless packets when the corresponding Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) entry was incomplete rather than falling back to flooding.

Systems lesson: PFC backpressure is a structural vulnerability of lossless Ethernet at hyperscale. RoCE fabrics require packet-class discipline, PFC pause telemetry, and hardware-level safeguards because a mechanism designed to avoid packet loss can itself become the failure mode. ML training fabrics built on RoCE inherit this risk directly. A single incomplete ARP entry can freeze the lossless class that carries a multi-thousand-GPU training run’s gradients, with the cluster appearing healthy while no gradient flows between workers.

Guo, Chuanxiong, Haitao Wu, Zhong Deng, Gaurav Soni, Jianxi Ye, Jitendra Padhye, and Marina Lipshteyn. 2016. “RDMA over Commodity Ethernet at Scale.” Proceedings of the 2016 ACM SIGCOMM Conference, 202–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/2934872.2934908.

PFC storms and victim flows

In contrast to a static topological cycle, a PFC storm is a dynamic, runaway amplification phenomenon triggered when a single “slow drainer” (an accelerator stalled by memory bus saturation, operating system preemption, or a degraded transceiver with elevated bit error rates) fails to consume packets at wire speed. The attached ToR switch queue fills and sends a PAUSE frame to the sender. Because multi-port switches aggregate traffic from many sources across shared packet buffers, the paused queue blocks buffer allocation for unrelated packets (head-of-line blocking). The spine switches feeding that ToR then fill their buffers and pause the other leaf switches that send through them, and each additional tier widens the set of paused links until traffic between node pairs that never communicated with the stalled worker also stops. These victim flows turn one slow endpoint into a job-wide barrier delay, because every data-parallel worker whose path crosses a paused spine port now arrives late at the barrier. Because a degraded transceiver is one such slow drainer, a component-exposure calculation shows how often a cluster should expect at least one, even though exposure alone does not estimate the probability of a PFC storm.

Napkin Math 3.4: Probability of at least one transceiver degradation
Problem: A 4096-GPU RoCE cluster, larger than the reference cluster, is in operation. If the probability that a given transceiver begins degrading on a given day is 0.001 percent, what is the chance that at least one transceiver degrades on a given day?

Math: Assume independent transceiver-degradation events with the same per-device probability.

  1. Total exposure: 4096 \(\times\) 3 link tiers = 12,288 links, or 24,576 transceivers.
  2. Probability of zero transceiver degradations: \(\Pr(\text{no degradation}) = (1 - 0.00001)^{24,576} \approx 0.782\).
  3. Probability of at least one degradation: \(\Pr(\text{at least one degradation}) = 1 - 0.782 = \mathbf{0.218}\) (about 21.8 percent).

Systems insight: Under these assumptions, a large RoCE cluster has a 21.8 percent daily chance of at least one degraded transceiver. This result motivates hardware telemetry and automated remediation, but estimating fabric-wide freeze risk additionally requires the conditional probability that a degradation triggers and propagates a PFC storm.

Watchdog recovery and the lossy alternative

Switches can use PFC watchdog timers to detect prolonged pause states and trigger recovery without a full reboot. The configured threshold \(\PfcWatchdogTimeout\) must fit the deployment’s pause and recovery budgets; a range such as \(50\text{--}200\text{ ms}\) is a configuration example, not a universal requirement. Exceeding it indicates stalled progress, not proof of a cyclic deadlock. Depending on the implementation, mitigation can disable pause handling or discard affected traffic so blocked queues can drain. The transport must then recover any lost data, with the completion penalty determined by retransmission behavior, outstanding traffic, and the collective’s dependencies. On an RNIC with Go-Back-N-style recovery (section 3.4.3), each discarded packet also costs the in-flight window behind it, so breaking a pause converts a stalled queue into a retransmission tail that the waiting collective absorbs.

This systemic brittleness is driving hyperscale operators toward next-generation architectures such as the Ultra Ethernet Consortium (UEC) (Ultra Ethernet Consortium 2025), detailed in section 3.10.1, and loss-tolerant RDMA designs (Mittal et al. 2018). Designs that combine packet spraying with congestion control and selective retransmission can operate without link-layer PFC. Removing PFC removes its pause-based deadlocks and storms, but endpoint recovery must still bound the completion-time penalty of congestion and loss.

Mittal, Radhika, Alexander Shpiner, Aurojit Panda, Eitan Zahavi, Arvind Krishnamurthy, Sylvia Ratnasamy, and Scott Shenker. 2018. “Revisiting Network Support for RDMA.” Proceedings of the 2018 Conference of the ACM Special Interest Group on Data Communication, 313–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3230543.3230557.

3.6.3 End-to-end congestion control with DCQCN and HPCC

Because link-layer PFC fires only when a switch buffer is already near exhaustion, relying on pauses alone invites the cascades and watchdog drops of section 3.6.2. Keeping queues below those thresholds requires end-to-end congestion control, which throttles sender injection rates before buffers fill. For a BSP workload with a completion-time target, this rate control is a latency requirement rather than an optional throughput optimization: delaying a required transfer extends the synchronization boundary, so the controller must balance delivered throughput against queueing and recovery tails.

The first widely deployed solution, DCQCN,8 operates as a reactive feedback loop using ECN (Zhu et al. 2015). When a switch’s queue depth exceeds a configured threshold, it marks the ECN bit in the packet header. The receiver echoes this mark back to the sender via a congestion notification packet (CNP), prompting the sender to reduce its injection rate using a multiplicative-decrease algorithm. DCQCN is widely supported, but production AI collectives expose a tuning problem because the feedback loop takes a full network round trip to take effect. Setting the ECN threshold high leaves too little buffer headroom for synchronized bursts to slow down before hitting the PFC pause threshold, whereas setting it low causes senders to throttle aggressively on brief queue spikes and underutilize the link. Consequently, ECN thresholds and NIC firmware behavior trade off PFC avoidance, throughput, visibility, and tail latency rather than offering one stable optimum (Gangidi et al. 2024).

8 DCQCN (data center quantized congestion notification): Introduced by Zhu et al. (2015) (Microsoft and Mellanox) at SIGCOMM 2015 for large-scale RDMA fabrics, its binary ECN/CNP feedback loop is simpler to deploy than telemetry-rich schemes. However, it creates a delicate tuning surface for training collectives: Meta’s 2024 RoCE deployment showed that aggressive ECN marking reduces PFC pauses but degrades collective completion time (Gangidi et al. 2024).

Zhu, Y., H. Eran, D. Firestone, C. Guo, M. Lipshteyn, Y. Liron, J. Padhye, S. Raindel, M. H. Yahia, and M. Zhang. 2015. “Congestion Control for Large-Scale RDMA Deployments.” ACM SIGCOMM Computer Communication Review 45 (4): 523–36. https://doi.org/10.1145/2829988.2787484.
Gangidi, Adi, Rui Miao, Sandeep Hebbani, Gaya Nagarajan, Omar Baldonado, Lixin Gao, Hany Morsy Goes, et al. 2024. “RDMA over Ethernet for Distributed AI Training at Meta Scale.” Proceedings of the ACM SIGCOMM 2024 Conference, 56–69. https://doi.org/10.1145/3651890.3672233.

9 HPCC (high precision congestion control): Introduced by Li et al. (2019) (Alibaba) at SIGCOMM 2019, HPCC requires programmable switch ASICs that append INT metadata to packets at line rate, a hardware dependency that limits the fabrics able to deploy it.

Li, Yuliang, Rui Miao, Hongqiang Harry Liu, Yan Zhuang, Fei Feng, Lingbo Tang, Zheng Cao, et al. 2019. “HPCC: High Precision Congestion Control.” Proceedings of the ACM Special Interest Group on Data Communication (SIGCOMM), 44–58. https://doi.org/10.1145/3341302.3342085.

HPCC9 uses in-network telemetry (INT) to give the sender more information about path load (Li et al. 2019). Switch measurements of queue occupancy and transmitted traffic let the controller estimate an appropriate injection rate rather than react only to a binary congestion mark. That information still arrives after a feedback delay, so the controller must account for changing load and avoid overreaction. The evaluated design reduces queue buildup and tail latency, but requires switch telemetry support and compatible endpoint processing. DCQCN needs less detailed switch feedback, trading that simpler deployment requirement against a different tuning problem.

3.6.4 Adaptive routing

Throttling senders with DCQCN or HPCC relieves a filling queue only by slowing the flows that cross it, even when parallel spine links sit idle. That imbalance is the ECMP hash-collision problem from section 3.2: because static flow hashing,10 which works well for millions of small flows, pins each persistent elephant flow to a fixed path, an 8-flow AllReduce across 4 equal-cost paths can place 4 flows on one link while another carries none. In a synchronized training step, that overloaded link then determines completion time and can halve useful bandwidth.

10 ECMP (equal-cost multi-path): ECMP selects a path by hashing on the 5-tuple (source/destination IP, source/destination port, protocol), which means the same flow always takes the same path. This determinism is a feature for packet ordering but a liability for ML. Because a single AllReduce ring produces a small number of persistent flows, hash collisions are not transient statistical events but hot spots that last as long as those flows do.

11 Packet spraying: Unlike standard Ethernet (which hashes flows to single paths to avoid reordering), packet spraying sends individual packets of a single flow across multiple paths. This can improve use of the available \(\BisectionBandwidth\) by AllReduce elephant flows when the transport can tolerate or repair reordering. The Ultra Ethernet Consortium (UEC) specification design explicitly includes multi-path packet spraying and flexible ordering to reduce the ECMP collision problem for AI/HPC traffic (Ultra Ethernet Consortium 2025).

Ultra Ethernet Consortium. 2025. Ultra Ethernet Specification V1.0. Ultra Ethernet Consortium.

Adaptive routing mitigates this problem by allowing switches to select among eligible output paths using congestion information rather than only a static hash. The implementation is protocol- and device-dependent. Packet-level spraying11 can send packets of one connection over different paths, so the receiver may observe reordering. Compatible endpoint support must accept out-of-order placement or restore the transport’s required ordering; the presence of InfiniBand alone does not guarantee arbitrary spraying across every path.

Ethernet fabrics can also employ flowlet switching, which changes a flow’s path between bursts separated by a gap large enough to preserve packet order across the candidate paths, so its usefulness depends on how often the workload leaves such gaps relative to path delay differences. MoE expert exchanges illustrate why path diversity matters: a dense exchange among 64 expert-parallel ranks contains at most \(64(64-1)=4{,}032\) directed remote rank pairs after excluding self-transfers, with active traffic governed by expert placement and token-routing sparsity. Where static-hash collisions concentrate that traffic onto a few links, adaptive routing spreads it across the provisioned \(\BisectionBandwidth\). Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) creates similar exchanges when batches of embedding requests and results move among table shards, although batch and shard placement determine the traffic matrix rather than every individual lookup requiring a global exchange.

3.6.5 The incast problem in ML

Congestion control and adaptive routing manage flows that traverse the interior of the fabric. A different failure mode occurs at the edge, where the traffic pattern itself overwhelms a single port regardless of how much capacity the fabric interior provides.

Definition 3.10: Incast

Incast is a many-to-one ML cluster traffic pattern in which a large number of senders simultaneously transmit data to a single receiver port. It concentrates line-rate traffic from multiple sources into a single switch queue, and it can overflow the buffer if the burst outlasts the available buffering and control response, even when the rest of the fabric is uncongested.

  1. Significance: Flat aggregation, synchronized storage reads, or other fan-in phases can direct many senders toward one receiver. The deliberately worst-case scenario here assumes a RoCE fabric whose ports all run at 400 Gb/s. In it, 256 senders each transmit at 50 GB/s toward one switch egress, producing 12.8 TB/s of offered load against a 400 Gb/s port. A 32 MB buffer then overflows in about 2.5 μs under the stated assumptions, after which the switch either drops packets or, in a PFC deployment, pauses its upstream neighbors. The example is a stress bound, not the traffic matrix of a ring AllReduce.
  2. Distinction: Unlike general congestion (which occurs on shared internal links when aggregate traffic exceeds link capacity), incast is an endpoint bottleneck. It occurs even if every internal spine and leaf link is completely uncongested, because the bottleneck is the single destination port, not the fabric interior.
  3. Common pitfall: Incast is not necessarily rare or unpredictable. Training jobs create it on a schedule, whenever many GPUs read dataset shards from or write checkpoints to the same storage node, and in the aggregation step of a tree or hierarchical reduction, where several ranks send partial sums to one parent. A gradient ring is different; its point-to-point flows can still collide at a shared egress, but that is a hot spot rather than many-to-one incast. The severity depends on the collective algorithm, topology, routing, and oversubscription, so it must be measured and mitigated rather than treated as an edge case.

In a worst-case incast, 12.8 TB/s of offered traffic from 256 senders converges on a single 400 Gb/s (50 GB/s) egress (figure 13). Each sender injects at 50 GB/s. The buffer fills over a finite interval; control feedback can reduce arrivals, drops can discard excess traffic, or backpressure can move the queueing upstream. Whether that pressure spreads depends on shared queues and the paths carrying the traffic. Let the aggregate arrival rate to one egress be \(\EgressArrivalRate\), the egress service rate be \(\EgressServiceRate\), and the instantaneous queue occupancy be \(\EgressQueueOccupancy(\TimeInstant)\). While a burst persists and \(\EgressArrivalRate > \EgressServiceRate\), the queue grows at the net rate

\[ \EgressQueueOccupancy(\TimeInstant) = \min\!\left(\EgressBufferCapacity,\; \EgressQueueOccupancy(0) + (\EgressArrivalRate-\EgressServiceRate)\TimeInstant\right), \]

where \(\EgressBufferCapacity\) is the finite buffer capacity. The ratio \(\EgressPortUtilization=\EgressArrivalRate/\EgressServiceRate\) distinguishes a drainable workload from sustained overload: for \(\EgressPortUtilization<1\), idle intervals can drain the queue; as long-run \(\EgressPortUtilization\) approaches one, less slack remains to absorb bursts; and for \(\EgressPortUtilization>1\), occupancy must reach a control threshold or the buffer limit if the overload persists. This is a packet queue, distinct from the request queues of an inference server. Serialization and propagation set the unloaded path latency, whereas queued bytes add approximately \(\EgressQueueOccupancy/\EgressServiceRate\) of waiting time.

⇄ Contrast: Request queues, whose arrival and service rates set serving latency rather than switch buffer occupancy, are developed in Queuing theory for batched inference.

In the deliberately worst-case incast above, \(\EgressPortUtilization\) is 256×, so the 32 MB buffer fills in about 2.5 μs. Once full, that buffer represents 640 μs of egress waiting time. By contrast, the bandwidth-delay product for the 400 Gb/s egress and an illustrative 5 μs feedback round trip is only 250 KB; it measures data in flight on the path, not the buffering needed to absorb an arbitrary fan-in burst. ECN must mark early enough for feedback to arrive before exhaustion; PFC can prevent a drop, but its backpressure can spread congestion. A larger buffer extends the reaction window at the cost of a larger tail-latency bound.

Synchronized ML traffic is susceptible to both true fan-in and shared-egress hot spots. When a layer finishes backward computation, many ranks can inject traffic at once, and flat or hierarchical aggregation phases can create true fan-in. Production clusters mitigate both cases along three complementary dimensions:

  1. Time. Layer-staggering triggers each gradient bucket’s AllReduce as soon as backpropagation produces it. Final-layer gradients reduce while earlier layers are still computing, so communication is spread across the backward pass instead of released as one synchronized burst. The same staggering is communication-computation overlap, so it also hides transfer time behind the backward computation that remains.
  2. Space. Collective algorithm selection bounds fan-in at individual switch ports. Hierarchical and rail-local reductions aggregate gradients in stages so that no single port must absorb all senders at once, and topology-aware placement keeps concurrent ring flows off oversubscribed ports.
  3. Priority. Quality of service (QoS) classification tags gradient traffic with the highest service class to reduce interference from background storage or management traffic; shared links, buffers, and arbitration still constrain isolation.

These mitigations spread the burst in time, space, and priority, but they do not eliminate congestion or tail latency. The question becomes how to build end-to-end clusters that perform despite these realities.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a RoCEv2 fabric, what is ‘congestion spreading’, which stalls victim flows, and what mechanism causes it?

    1. Optical dispersion in fiber cables causing packet corruption across adjacent wavelength channels
    2. High ambient temperatures in switch chassis causing SerDes circuits to throttle line rates across all ports
    3. Software hypervisors duplicating tenant packets across multiple Virtual Functions
    4. Priority Flow Control (PFC) PAUSE frames propagating upstream from a single congested egress port, filling upstream switch buffers and pausing unrelated flows that share those intermediate switches

  2. How does High Precision Congestion Control (HPCC) achieve faster convergence and lower queue buildup during incast bursts compared to DCQCN?

    1. HPCC drops all out-of-order packets at the edge switch and forces senders to switch to TCP cubic
    2. HPCC uses In-Network Telemetry (INT) to append precise switch queue depth, link utilization, and timestamps to packet headers, allowing senders to estimate available capacity and adjust rates rapidly
    3. HPCC disables Priority Flow Control and relies exclusively on application-level checkpointing to tolerate packet loss
    4. HPCC increases switch buffer sizes to \(10\text{ GB}\) per port to eliminate the possibility of buffer overflows

  3. During a synchronized fan-in phase such as flat gradient aggregation, describe the physical conditions that create the ‘incast problem’ at a switch port, and calculate the time required to overflow a \(32\text{ MB}\) switch buffer when 256 GPUs each transmit at \(50\text{ GB/s}\) toward a single \(50\text{ GB/s}\) (\(400\text{ Gb/s}\)) egress port.

  4. Why does packet spraying improve fabric bisection bandwidth utilization in InfiniBand networks, and why is standard Ethernet historically hesitant to use it?

    1. Packet spraying distributes individual packets of an elephant flow across all available equal-cost paths to eliminate hash collisions; suitable InfiniBand implementations support this through routing and endpoint ordering mechanisms, whereas standard Ethernet avoids reordering due to the high software/NIC cost of reassembly
    2. Packet spraying encrypts packets with unique keys across paths, which InfiniBand decrypts in hardware while Ethernet lacks AES accelerators
    3. Packet spraying routes packets over wireless backup links, which Ethernet switches do not support
    4. Packet spraying reduces packet header size from 40 bytes to 4 bytes, doubling Ethernet wire efficiency

  5. Place the following events in order to illustrate how an incast burst escalates into a cluster-wide PFC congestion storm in a RoCEv2 fabric:

  1. The downstream switch buffer reaches its high-water mark and emits PFC PAUSE frames upstream
  2. Upstream switches pause their transmission queues, causing their own ingress buffers to fill with traffic destined for other ports
  3. Multiple GPU senders simultaneously initiate flat-aggregation transfers targeting a common aggregation node
  4. Second-tier upstream switches emit PAUSE frames to unrelated sender nodes, freezing victim flows across the fabric
  5. The common destination switch egress queue is oversubscribed by the combined line-rate traffic

See Answers →

3.7 Level 5 Cluster Design and Case Studies

The final level of the stack is cluster design, where wires, transport, topology, and congestion control combine into a coherent system. The goal of cluster design is to provide an end-to-end gradient bus that makes thousands of distributed GPUs feel like a single machine. At this level, the abstraction layers collapse into concrete engineering decisions: which cables to buy, how to wire the racks, which protocol to deploy, and how to validate that the resulting fabric delivers the bandwidth the training job expects. Two representative large-scale architectures, one built on InfiniBand and one on Ethernet, illuminate the trade-offs that define ML infrastructure (NVIDIA 2023; Meta Engineering 2024; Gangidi et al. 2024).

3.7.1 The GPU-to-GPU “gradient bus”

In a well-designed cluster, the network fabric acts as a scheduler-aware extension of the system bus, not a passive pipe. The ~9× per-direction cliff between NVLink and one external port, measured at the start of this chapter, is the gap the cluster must hide. Communication-computation overlap, which the Fleet Law credits as \(\OverlappedTime\), is the first mechanism for hiding it, through the same layer-staggered AllReduce launch that spreads incast bursts in section 3.6.5. Overlap cannot hide the last mile of the exchange, because the gradients computed at the end of backpropagation have little computation left behind which to hide their transfer (Communication-Computation Overlap).

Placement is the second mechanism, because it decides which communication crosses each boundary. The placement that the bandwidth hierarchy of Bandwidth hierarchy sets, tensor parallelism inside the NVLink domain with pipeline stages and data-parallel replicas spanning nodes, becomes a wiring rule at cluster scale, because each fabric tier must be provisioned for the traffic placed on it. The links between nodes must meet the pipeline’s activation-transfer budget, which physical adjacency alone does not guarantee. The full fabric carries the data-parallel exchanges, which gradient accumulation makes less frequent and overlap partly hides. Production designs therefore differ mainly in how they provision that outermost tier, as the two that follow show.

3.7.2 NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD architecture

The NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD, a concrete implementation of the gradient bus, provisions the outermost tier by obtaining losslessness from the link layer and aligning the wiring with the data-parallel ranks, so that the fabric’s tail behavior depends on hardware mechanisms rather than on congestion tuning. It connects DGX H100 nodes using an NDR InfiniBand network (NVIDIA 2023). Each node acts as a dense compute island, with eight H100 GPUs connected via NVSwitch to provide 900 GB/s of internal bandwidth. Externally, each GPU pairs with a ConnectX-7 NIC delivering 400 Gb/s of injection bandwidth. Across a standard scalable unit of 32 nodes (256 GPUs), this yields an aggregate injection bandwidth of 12.8 TB/s (256 \(\times\) 50 GB/s), the rate the scalable unit can offer the fabric when every GPU transmits at once.

NVIDIA. 2023. NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD: Next Generation Scalable Infrastructure for AI Leadership. RA-11333-001 V11. NVIDIA.

This architecture connects all five levels of the network model into a single chain. At the wire (Level 1), it standardizes on 400 Gb/s NDR links, choosing copper or optics by the reach between each node and its leaf and between leaf and spine. Because those links run InfiniBand’s native credit-based flow control at the transport layer (Level 2), they provide a lossless medium without the pause-cascade fragility of Ethernet PFC. Above the transport, the topology (Level 3) arranges the switches as a rail-optimized fat-tree, aligning GPU rails across each scalable unit so that same-rail traffic stays close while cross-rail traffic traverses the spine layer (replacing the simpler per-rack ToR allocation used earlier to illustrate switch radix). When cross-rank traffic climbs to the spine, hardware-based adaptive routing (Level 4) selects among spine paths to preserve bisection behavior. Finally, at the cluster level (Level 5), additional switching joins multiple SuperPOD scalable units into larger fleets (NVIDIA 2023): for the 175B-parameter model, four SuperPOD scalable units wire together so that 1,024 GPUs function as a single synchronous instrument.

3.7.3 Meta Grand Teton platform

Meta’s Grand Teton clusters take the opposite approach, accepting Ethernet’s congestion-tuning burden in exchange for multi-vendor supply and shared operations. Meta disclosed two Grand Teton-based 24,576-H100 clusters: one using RoCE over an Arista/OCP Ethernet fabric and one using NVIDIA Quantum-2 InfiniBand, both with 400 Gb/s endpoints. Meta used both for Llama 3 training and reported ongoing Llama 3 training on the RoCE cluster (Meta Engineering 2024). Ethernet lets Meta source switches from multiple vendors and reuse the optical infrastructure and management tooling of its front-end serving fleet, avoiding the operational silo of a dedicated InfiniBand island.

Meta Engineering. 2024. Building Meta’s GenAI Infrastructure. Engineering at Meta Blog.

Meta’s RoCE study shows how Level 4 choices affect the complete fabric. It describes PFC watchdogs for long-duration pause events, iterative routing designs beyond plain ECMP, future-oriented flowlet switching experiments, and a 400G deployment in which DCQCN tuning proved difficult enough that Meta proceeded without DCQCN while relying on PFC plus higher-layer collective controls (Gangidi et al. 2024). The lesson is to validate the combination of endpoint behavior, routing, and flow control under the intended collective traffic. Large-transfer throughput alone does not establish small-message latency or recovery tails, and those results cannot be inferred from the Ethernet or InfiniBand label. Small-message latency matters in training through frequent small exchanges, which expose startup cost even when a full step lasts seconds, and in serving to a degree set by model partitioning, request shape, and service-level objectives (SLOs).

These case studies close the five-level stack by showing how the physical wire, transport protocol, topology, and congestion controls become one production fabric. The remaining operational problem is not how to build a single fast cluster, but how to share that expensive substrate across teams and workloads without sacrificing the predictable performance that training demands.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a 3D-parallel training architecture (combining Tensor, Pipeline, and Data Parallelism) across a cluster of 8-GPU nodes, how should each parallelism dimension be mapped to the network hierarchy to optimize communication efficiency?

    1. Data Parallelism within the node over NVLink, Pipeline Parallelism across the leaf switches, and Tensor Parallelism across the core spine switches
    2. Tensor Parallelism within the node over high-bandwidth NVLink, Pipeline Parallelism across immediate inter-node links, and Data Parallelism across the cluster fabric with backward-pass overlap
    3. Tensor Parallelism across the multi-hop core network, Data Parallelism within the CPU socket, and Pipeline Parallelism over PCIe storage buses
    4. All three parallelism dimensions mapped uniformly across all network tiers using round-robin flow allocation

  2. Why can communication-computation overlap not hide the last mile of the gradient exchange in distributed training, and what does that leave on the critical path?

  3. What is the primary operational and architectural motivation for Meta adopting RoCEv2 over commodity Ethernet in its Grand Teton clusters rather than dedicated InfiniBand?

    1. RoCEv2 provides lower physical-layer forward error correction latency than InfiniBand
    2. Ethernet cabling is physically immune to transceiver symbol errors
    3. Commodity Ethernet enables multi-vendor switch and optics sourcing, supply chain resilience, and unified management tooling with web serving infrastructure, despite requiring careful PFC and routing tuning
    4. InfiniBand switches cannot support link rates exceeding \(100\text{ Gb/s}\)

  4. True or False: An NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD scalable unit eliminates inter-node optical transceiver costs by connecting all 32 DGX nodes in the scalable unit using passive direct-attach copper cables to the core spine switches.

See Answers →

3.8 Network Virtualization

When a single training job leaves racks or spine links idle between runs, operators carve the cluster among multiple tenants and jobs, only to discover that a neighbor’s checkpoint burst or evaluation sweep can starve a synchronous AllReduce across shared NICs and switch buffers. Network virtualization must make sharing compatible with bandwidth allocations, latency targets, and access isolation. These requirements interact because tenants still share physical links and buffers. A proposed policy might reserve 80 percent of the cluster’s \(\BisectionBandwidth\) for training and allocate the remaining 20 percent to inference and preprocessing. Enforcing that policy requires admission control and compatible scheduling along the relevant paths; a virtual NIC or separate traffic class alone does not guarantee the end-to-end share.

3.8.1 Hardware NIC virtualization with SR-IOV

Routing multi-tenant traffic through a hypervisor’s software virtual switch reintroduces the exact host-CPU overhead and microsecond jitter that RDMA was built to bypass. Cloud providers instead expose NIC resources to virtualized GPU instances through single-root I/O virtualization (SR-IOV), a standard that allows a physical NIC to present multiple virtual functions (VFs). Each VF exposes its own hardware queues, doorbell registers, and DMA mappings, which software can assign to a workload. A directly assigned VF can remove host software mediation from the active DMA path, while drivers and virtualization software still configure access and manage the device. Performance depends on the NIC, I/O placement, and competing traffic. Because VFs share physical resources, placement logic must reserve NIC capacity and QoS policy alongside GPUs rather than treat the network as an unbounded pool.

However, hardware-level isolation still needs explicit bandwidth policy. SR-IOV exposes multiple VFs that share the NIC’s physical resources; administrators can add per-VF or per-group QoS policies to cap or guarantee bandwidth, such as assigning eight VFs 50 Gb/s each on a 400 Gb/s NIC. For the 175B-parameter model training on a multi-tenant cluster, that configured partitioning can prevent a neighboring tenant from stealing bandwidth, but it also enforces a hard ceiling on peak throughput. The training job must be architected to operate within this slice, as no amount of software optimization can burst beyond the configured VF limit.

3.8.2 Traffic isolation and quality of service

Per-VF rate caps bound what each tenant injects at the NIC, but traffic from different tenants still merges on the fabric’s shared switch buffers and spine links. When the 175B model is midway through a gradient AllReduce and a neighboring job initiates a 100 GB checkpoint save, that burst occupies a 400 Gb/s port for 2 s at full line rate. It can also claim a large share of the shared spine links for that interval, which stretches every AllReduce whose traffic crosses them. To prevent this noisy-neighbor effect inside the fabric, high-performance switches rely on quality of service (QoS) mechanisms that enforce fairness at the packet and queue level.

InfiniBand isolates traffic through virtual lanes (VLs), which maintain separate credit and buffer state within a physical link across up to 16 lanes (one of them reserved for subnet management) (InfiniBand Trade Association 2000). If a storage burst exhausts the credits of its own lane, a gradient lane with available credits can continue transmitting, even though both lanes still share link bandwidth and arbitration. Ethernet and RoCE fabrics achieve a similar separation through device-dependent traffic class mappings and queue policies, where enhanced transmission selection allocates service among traffic classes and switches may also partition buffer and forwarding resources into network slices. Across both stacks, these mechanisms reduce interference only when classification, resource allocation, and scheduling agree along the entire path, because shared priorities or buffer pools can still couple tenants.

Virtualization solves the sharing problem but makes performance diagnosis harder. When a training job slows down on a multi-tenant cluster, the cause could be a physical link degradation, a noisy neighbor exceeding its bandwidth allocation, or a misconfigured QoS policy. Systematic monitoring is essential to distinguish these cases.

Self-Check: Question

  1. How does Single Root I/O Virtualization (SR-IOV) enable near-bare-metal RDMA performance in multi-tenant cloud GPU environments?

    1. It allows a physical NIC to present multiple independent Virtual Functions (VFs) directly to guest VMs or containers, bypassing the host hypervisor and OS kernel for DMA operations
    2. It emulates a 10 GbE software NIC inside the KVM kernel module to buffer gradient bursts
    3. It compresses tenant network streams using the host CPU’s AVX-512 vector units before transmission
    4. It dynamically converts InfiniBand packets into TCP/IP frames at the virtual switch layer

  2. Explain how Virtual Lanes (VLs) in InfiniBand (or Traffic Classes in RoCEv2) reduce the impact of a multi-gigabyte storage checkpoint burst on the iteration time of an active distributed training job on a shared cluster.

  3. True or False: Configuring SR-IOV Virtual Functions (VFs) with strict bandwidth limits (such as capping each VF at \(50\text{ Gb/s}\) on a \(400\text{ Gb/s}\) NIC) allows a training job to dynamically burst up to \(400\text{ Gb/s}\) during AllReduce if other VFs on the host are idle.

  4. In InfiniBand fabrics, traffic isolation is achieved using independent logical channels called ______, which maintain separate credit-based buffer management on the same physical link.

3.9 Monitoring and Debugging

Network performance problems in ML clusters are insidious because they show up as silent waste rather than explicit failures. A degraded transceiver causing a 10 percent reduction in effective bandwidth might slow each training iteration by only 2–3 percent. The slowdown is small because the lost bandwidth stretches only the exposed communication share of the step (the 30 percent assumed in the oversubscription estimate, less whatever overlap hides), and stretches it by roughly the inverse of the bandwidth that remains. A drift that small is easily masked by the natural variance of checkpointing or data loading. Over a 30-day training run on 1,024 GPUs, this invisible drag accumulates to roughly 15,000–22,000 wasted GPU-hours, burning about $30,000–$44,000 at $2/GPU-hour without triggering a single alarm. Traditional IT monitoring tools like SNMP or ICMP ping measure connectivity, not the sustained throughput required by RDMA. Effective observability requires a three-layer approach: physical monitoring (FEC errors, signal attenuation), transport monitoring (PFC pause frames, retransmission rates), and application monitoring (NCCL algorithmic bandwidth) (Jeaugey 2017; Gangidi et al. 2024). The three layers retrace the five-level model from the top down. Application monitoring observes the collective that Level 5 integrates, transport monitoring observes the Level 2 recovery and Level 4 congestion machinery, and physical monitoring observes the Level 1 wire. Only by correlating signals across these layers can operators detect that a “slow training run” is caused by a single degraded cable in one rack.

Jeaugey, Sylvain. 2017. NCCL 2.0. GPU Technology Conference presentation.

3.9.1 Link-level telemetry

A marginal cable or overheating transceiver rarely drops the link outright; instead, it accumulates symbol errors, triggers transmit discards, or retrains to a slower link state while the job keeps running. Catching that physical drift requires polling switch and NIC port counters, which report transmitted and received data, transmit discards, symbol errors, and link-down events; on InfiniBand a utility such as perfquery reads them, and Ethernet/RoCE devices expose the equivalent telemetry through their own management interfaces (Linux man-pages project 2026). Data counters measure utilization, discards warrant investigation against the port’s traffic and recovery policy, and symbol errors and link-down events are evidence of a degrading signal. Correlating those counters with link rate, lane state, and transport behavior narrows the cause; a counter alone does not identify a defective cable or transceiver.

Linux man-pages project. 2026. Perfquery(8): Query InfiniBand Port Counters on a Single Port.

Polling link state alongside traffic counters can reveal silent degradation. A link operating at HDR (200 Gb/s) rather than the expected NDR rate (400 Gb/s) can remain connected while offering half the expected bandwidth. A link that trains with two of its four lanes active after a connector fault fails the same way: it stays up while its bandwidth is halved. Both cases are visible only when negotiated rate and lane state are compared with the deployment baseline. For 1,024 GPUs and roughly 3,000 links, the assumed 0.1 percent point-in-time degradation probability gives 3 degraded links in expectation and, under independent events, a 95 percent probability of at least one degradation. This probability measures something different from the daily figure in notebook 3.4. That notebook asks how often a new degradation begins; this one asks how many degraded links are present at any moment. Standing prevalence is roughly the onset rate times how long a degraded link stays in service, and silent degradation, by definition, stays in service until someone looks, so prevalence can far exceed what a small daily onset rate suggests. Both rates are scenario assumptions rather than measured fleet data. A job slows when an affected path limits its required traffic; fleet-wide exposure alone does not determine the job’s slowdown. When the degraded link lies on a ring AllReduce’s path, however, the cost reaches every participant, because each round waits for its slowest hop and the whole ring proceeds at the pace of that one link.

↪ Gray failure, the general case of a component that degrades without failing, and why a synchronous job feels it as a straggler, is developed in Stragglers versus fail-stop failures.

3.9.2 Bandwidth and latency validation

Clean port counters and an active 50 GB/s NDR link state still do not guarantee that a node pair can deliver that rate to a training job: a degraded PCIe path, a cross-socket NUMA bounce, or a congested spine cut can throttle RDMA transfers without incrementing a single physical error counter. Active validation must therefore measure the bandwidth and latency the job can actually achieve across the complete host-to-host path. Pairwise tests such as ib_write_bw can populate an all-pairs bandwidth matrix, or a sampled subset for a large fleet (linux-rdma project 2026). A low-throughput pair identifies a path to investigate. Comparing the affected pairs with routing and physical telemetry helps distinguish a shared switch or cable problem from endpoint limitations.

linux-rdma project. 2026. perftest: InfiniBand Verbs Performance Tests.

For latency-sensitive synchronization, ib_write_lat runs a ping-pong test with small RDMA writes and reports half the measured round-trip time as one-way latency. Values such as 2 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s, 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s, or 100 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s must be compared with a baseline for the same endpoints, message size, path, and load; they are not universal fault thresholds or evidence of a particular fallback. Application-level tests such as nccl-tests then exercise collective communication across the intended worker group. Point-to-point results establish a path baseline, while collective tests reveal costs from concurrent traffic and scheduling that isolated pairs cannot expose.

3.9.3 Systematic debugging workflow

When a training job reports lower-than-expected throughput, the diagnostic order should preserve the layer model rather than starting with cables. The following sequence moves from application symptoms toward physical causes:

  1. Check GPU utilization: Rule out compute bottlenecks with accelerator telemetry (nvidia-smi, for example). High SM utilization alone does not rule out a network bottleneck, because communication kernels can also keep SMs active. Correlate utilization with the compute and collective portions of the step timeline. Low utilization does not automatically implicate the fabric, however. A starved input pipeline (data loading or storage) produces the same symptom, so confirm the data path is keeping the accelerators fed before investigating the network.
  2. Inspect collective-library logs: Enable the library’s debug output (NCCL_DEBUG=INFO in NCCL, for example) to reveal which network transport was selected, the detected bandwidth between nodes, and any fallbacks to slower protocols.
  3. Run point-to-point tests: Run a pairwise bandwidth test (such as ib_write_bw) between specific nodes in the job. A single degraded link can bottleneck the entire ring in a ring AllReduce.
  4. Check PFC/ECN counters: Inspect switch counters along the path. Sustained PFC activity indicates persistent congestion that should be investigated at the scheduler or routing level.
  5. Validate physical layer: Check symbol errors and CRC counts to identify failing transceivers or cables.

In this order, application symptoms identify the failing path, transport counters show whether congestion or fallback is involved, and physical telemetry confirms whether the wire itself is degrading. Applying the sequence to a concrete slowdown shows how each layer narrows the search before any hardware is replaced.

Checkpoint 3.5: Diagnosing a training slowdown

Your 175B model training job has been running for 3 days on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. You notice that the iteration time has gradually increased from 4.2 s to 4.8 s (a 14 percent slowdown). The GPU utilization reported by nvidia-smi has dropped from 92 percent to 85 percent.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A training job has slowed by a few percent. On one link, the port counters show a rising symbol-error count, and its data counters show lower throughput than links serving comparable traffic. What does this counter evidence establish on its own?

    1. The transceiver on that link is defective and must be replaced before the job resumes
    2. The link is a candidate cause, to be confirmed by correlating its negotiated rate, lane state, and transport behavior with the job’s slow collective
    3. The fabric is congested, so PFC and ECN thresholds must be retuned
    4. Nothing useful, because port counters cannot reflect training performance

  2. Explain the concept of ‘silent degradation’ in an AI cluster fabric, give a concrete physical example of how it occurs, and describe its economic impact on a large synchronous training run.

  3. What is the primary purpose of constructing an ‘All-Pairs Bandwidth Matrix’ before launching a distributed training job?

    1. To run point-to-point bandwidth benchmarks (such as ib_write_bw) across all node pairs and visualize a heatmap of underperforming node pairs that warrant investigation of paths and endpoints
    2. To pre-allocate GPU memory buffers for all possible tensor-parallel communication patterns
    3. To calculate the exact gradient loss trajectory of the neural network architecture
    4. To calibrate the clock frequency of the CPU PCIe root complex

  4. Order the recommended diagnostic steps when investigating an unexpected slowdown in a multi-node GPU training job, moving from application symptoms down to physical causes:

  1. Inspect switch PFC pause and ECN congestion counters along the network path
  2. Run point-to-point RDMA bandwidth tests (ib_write_bw) between candidate nodes
  3. Check GPU compute and SM utilization (using nvidia-smi or dcgmi)
  4. Validate physical layer health by checking symbol errors and CRC discard counters
  5. Enable NCCL debug logging (NCCL_DEBUG=INFO) to identify transport selection and detected topology
  1. True or False: In a healthy, properly configured lossless InfiniBand or RoCEv2 fabric, the PortXmitDiscards counter on switch ports should periodically increment under heavy AllReduce traffic to signal senders to back off.
